## Fourth BOOK
->OF<-
# Occult Philosophy.

### Regarding GEOMANCY. <note>original: "Of Geomancy" — a method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand.</note> —

### MAGICAL ELEMENTS by *Peter de Abano*. <note>original: "Peter de Abano" — an Italian philosopher and astrologer (c. 1250–1316); this text is often titled the "Heptameron," a manual of ritual magic.</note>

### ASTRONOMICAL GEOMANCY.
->By *Gerard of Cremona*<- <note>original: "Gerard Cremonensis" — a 12th-century scholar famous for translating scientific and occult works from Arabic into Latin.</note>

### ON THE NATURE OF SPIRITS. <note>original: "Of the Nature of Spirits"</note>
->By *Georg Pictorius of Villingen*<- <note>original: "G. P. Villinganus" — a 16th-century German physician who wrote on the classification of spirits and the dangers of magic.</note>

### ARBATEL *of* MAGIC. . . . <note>The "Arbatel de Magia Veterum" is a celebrated 16th-century grimoire focused on spiritual advice and the "Olympian spirits."</note>

# The PREFACE
## To the Unprejudiced READER.

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'A' set within a square woodcut frame decorated with floral and foliate patterns.</image-desc>

*Just as the fall of man made himself and all other creatures subject to futility <note>original: "vanity" — in the biblical sense of Ecclesiastes, meaning frailty, emptiness, or the loss of original perfection</note>, so, for that reason, the most noble and excellent arts with which the rational soul was endowed have been brought to corruption by the rusty decay of time.* For Magic itself, which the ancients contemplated so divinely, is disgraced by bearing the reputation <note>original: "badge"</note> of all diabolical sorceries. This is an *art* of which Mirandula <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Italian Renaissance philosopher who sought to reconcile magic and Christianity</note> says, *"Few understand it, many criticize it, and just as dogs always bark at strangers"* <note>original Latin: "Pauci intelligunt, multi reprehendunt, & sicut canes ignotos semper allatrant"</note>. Few understand it, and many condemn it; and as dogs bark at those they do not know, so many people condemn and hate the things they do not understand. There are many men who abhor the very name and word *Magus* <term>Magician or Wise Man</term> because of *Simon Magus* <note>A biblical figure in the Book of Acts associated with sorcery and the sin of "simony"</note>. He was not actually a *Magus*, but a *Goes* <term>a sorcerer or charlatan dealing in "low" or black magic</term>—meaning someone familiar with evil spirits—who stole that title.

<margin>Pliny, Book 30 of *Natural History*.</margin>
But Magic and witchcraft are very different sciences. Pliny, being ignorant of this, scoffs at it; for Nero (according to *Pliny*), who had the most excellent magicians of the East sent to him by Tiridates, King of Armenia (who held that kingdom under him), found the *art* completely ridiculous after long study and labor. Now, witchcraft and sorcery are works performed solely by the Devil. In accordance with some covenant <note>original: "covenant" — a formal pact or agreement with a demonic entity</note> made with a human, he acts through men as his instruments to accomplish his evil ends. The histories of all ages, peoples, and countries, as well as the Holy Scriptures, provide us with various examples of these.

But *Magus* is originally a Persian word, used to describe someone who is entirely well-versed in things divine,

<summary>In this preface, the author defends the reputation of Magic by distinguishing it from diabolical sorcery and witchcraft, arguing that the term "Magus" has been unfairly maligned due to historical misunderstandings and figures like Simon Magus.</summary>
<keywords>Magic, Witchcraft, Sorcery, Magus, Simon Magus, Pliny the Elder, Pico della Mirandola, Tiridates of Armenia, Demonology, Renaissance Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, moving from a general defense of magic to defining the term "Magus" through the perspectives of various ancient cultures and distinguishing between divine magic and forbidden sorcery.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

...divine, and as *Plato* affirms <note>original: "affirmeth"</note>, the art of *Magic* is the art of worshipping God. The *Persians* called their gods
<margin>
*Magi* <note>original Greek: "Ma$\gamma\epsilon\varsigma$’s"</note>:
He who is a god by nature, or a servant of the gods <note>original Greek: "$\dot{o} \ \kappa\alpha\tau\alpha \ \phi\upsilon\sigma\iota\nu \ \Theta\epsilon\grave{o}\varsigma, \ \eta \ \theta\epsilon\rho\alpha\pi\epsilon\upsilon\tau\grave{\eta}\varsigma \ \tau\tilde{\omega}\nu \ \Theta\epsilon\tilde{\omega}\nu$"</note>
</margin>
*Magi*. Regarding this, *Apollonius* <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a first-century Greek philosopher often compared to Jesus for his reported miracles</note> says that a *Magus* is either a god by nature or a servant of the gods <note>original Greek: "$\dot{o} \ \kappa\alpha\tau\alpha \ \phi\upsilon\sigma\iota\nu \ \Theta\epsilon\grave{o}\varsigma, \ \eta \ \theta\epsilon\rho\alpha\pi\epsilon\upsilon\tau\grave{\eta}\varsigma \ \tau\tilde{\omega}\nu \ \Theta\epsilon\tilde{\omega}\nu$"</note>. This means that *Magus* is a name sometimes used for one who is a god by nature, and sometimes for one who is in the service of God. It is used in this latter sense <note>original: "fence," an OCR error for "sence"</note> in Matthew 2:1, 2, when the wise men came to worship *Jesus*. This is the first and highest kind of magic, which is called divine *Magic*. The *Latins* entitled <note>original: "intitle"</note> these people *Sapientes*, or wise men; for the fear and worship of God is the beginning of knowledge.

The *Greeks* call these wise men *Philosophers*, and among the *Egyptians* they were termed *Priests*. The *Hebrews* called them *Cabalists*, *Prophets*, *Scribes*, and *Pharisees*; among the *Babylonians* they were distinguished by the name of *Chaldeans*, and the *Persians* called them *Magicians*. One author, speaking of *Sosthenes* <note>An ancient Persian magician mentioned in the writings of Pliny the Elder</note> (one of the ancient *Magicians*), uses these words: "He honors the true God with deserved majesty, and acknowledges that the angels are God's ministers, yet he knows how to assist in the worship of the true God; likewise, he reveals that there are demons who are earthly, wandering, and enemies to mankind" <note>original Latin: "Et verum Deum merita majestate prosequitur, & angelos ministros Dei, sed veri ejus venerationi novit assistere; idem dæmonas prodit terrenos, vagos, humanitatis inimicos"</note>. *Sosthenes* ascribes the due majesty to the true God and acknowledges that His *Angels* are ministers and messengers who attend the worship of the true God. He also has taught <note>original: "delivered"</note> that there are earthly and wandering devils who are enemies to mankind.

So the word *Magus* in itself implies a student <note>original: "Contemplator"</note> of divine and heavenly sciences. However, under the generic name of *Magic*, all unlawful arts are also included, such as *Necromancy* <term>the practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead</term> and *Witchcraft*, and such arts which are performed through a pact with the devil and in which he is a participant.

These *Witches* and *Necromancers* are also called *Malefici* <term>evildoers or performers of harmful magic</term> or *Venefici* <term>sorcerers or poisoners</term>. Witches are rightly called by these names because, without the Art of *Magic*, they do...

<meta>The text cuts off at "do," preparing for the next page's discussion on the actions of witches.</meta>

<summary>This page defines "Magic" in its highest sense as a divine art of worship practiced by ancient sages, contrasting it with the "unlawful arts" of necromancy and witchcraft which involve demonic pacts.</summary>
<keywords>Magus, divine magic, Sosthenes, Apollonius, Chaldeans, Cabalists, Necromancy, Witchcraft, Malefici, Venefici</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the definitions and types of Magic, distinguishing between corrupt practices and legitimate ancient sciences.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

<margin>Corrupt Magic</margin>
...do indeed use the help of the devil himself to do mischief; practicing the mixing of the powder of dead bodies with other things prepared by the help of the devil; and at other times making pictures of wax, clay, or otherwise—acting as if through a sacrament <note>original: *Sacramentaliter*</note>—to effect those things which the devil brings to pass by other means. Such were, and to this day partly (if not altogether) are the corruptions that have made the very name of **Magic** hateful. These practitioners have chiefly sought, as is the manner of all impostors, to counterfeit the highest and most noble part of it.

<margin>Astrology</margin>
A second kind of **Magic** is **Astrology**, which judges the events of things to come—both natural and human—by the motions and influences of the stars upon these lower elements, as observed and understood by the practitioners.

*Philo Judaeus* <note>A Hellenistic Jewish philosopher from Alexandria.</note> affirms that by this part of **Magic** or **Astrology**, together with the motions of the stars and other heavenly bodies, **Abraham** found out the knowledge of the true God while he lived in **Chaldea**. As *Damascene* <note>John of Damascus, a Syrian monk and scholar.</note> says, "He knew the Creator through the contemplation of the creature" <note>original: *Qui Contemplatione Creaturarum, cognovit Creatorem*</note>. *Josephus* reports of **Abraham** that he instructed the **Egyptians** in **Arithmetic** and **Astronomy**; before **Abraham's** coming to them, they knew none of these **Sciences**.

"Abraham, the most excellent of all men in holiness and wisdom, taught first the **Chaldeans**, then the **Phoenicians**, and finally the **Egyptian** Priests, **Astrology** and divine matters" <note>original: *Abraham sanctitate & sapientia omnium præ-stantissimus, primum Caldæos, deinde Phœnices, demum Egyptios Sacerdotes, Astrologia & Divina docuerit.*</note>.

Without doubt, **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the syncretism of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, credited with writing the Hermetic corpus.</note>, that divine Magician and Philosopher, who (as some say) lived long before **Noah**, attained to much divine knowledge of the Creator...

<summary>The author distinguishes between "corrupt magic" (necromancy and devil-worship) and "noble magic" (astrology and astronomy), arguing that biblical figures like Abraham used the study of the stars to discover the true God.</summary>
<keywords>Magic, Astrology, Abraham, Philo Judaeus, Hermes Trismegistus, Chaldea, Egypt, Astronomy, Natural Theology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the different types of Magic, specifically focusing on Natural Magic and the wisdom of Zoroaster.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

Creator through the study of *Magic* and *Astrology*; as his writings, which remain with us to this day, testify.

The third kind of *Magic* contains the whole *Philosophy of Nature*; which brings to light the innermost virtues, and extracts them out of *Nature's* hidden bosom for human use: "Virtues hidden in the center of the center," <note>original Latin: *Virtutes in centro centri latentes*</note> according to the *Chemists* <note>original: "Chymists," referring here to Alchemists</note>. Of this sort were *Albertus Magnus*, *Arnold of Villanova*, *Raymond Lully*, *Roger Bacon*, and others.

The *Magic* these men professed is defined as follows:
> *Magic is the connection of natural agents and patients <note>In this context, "patients" refers to things that receive an action or effect</note>, answerable to each other, wrought by a wise man to bring forth such effects as are wonderful to those who do not know their causes.* <note>original Latin: *Magia est connexio à viro sapiente agentium per naturam cum patientibus, sibi, congruenter respondentibus, ut inde opera prodeant, non sine eorum admiratione qui causam ignorant.*</note>

In all these, *Zoroaster* <note>An ancient Persian prophet and spiritual leader</note> was well-learned, especially in the first and the highest forms. For in his *Oracles* he confesses *God* to be the first and the highest; he believes in the *Trinity*, which he did not seek to investigate by any natural knowledge alone. He speaks of *Angels* and of *Paradise*; approves of the immortality of the soul; and teaches *Truth*, *Faith*, *Hope*, and *Love*, while discoursing on the abstinence and charity of the *Magi* <note>The priestly class of ancient Persia, from which the word "magic" is derived</note>.

Regarding this *Zoroaster*, *Eusebius* <note>A 4th-century Christian historian</note> writes in the *Theology of the Phoenicians*, using *Zoroaster's* own words:
> *He writes these things word for word* (says *Eusebius*): *God is the first of incorruptible things, eternal, unbegotten, without parts, most like unto himself, the driver of all good things, not expecting gifts, the best, the most prudent, the father of right, having learned justice without being taught, perfect by nature, wise, the sole inventor of sacred nature, etc.* <note>original Latin: *Hæc ad verbum scribit... Deus primus, incorruptibilium, sempiternus, ingenitus, expers partium, sibiipsi simillimus, bonorum omnium auriga, munera non expectans, optimus, prudentissimus, pater juris, sine doctrina justitiam perdoctus, natura perfectus, sapiens, sacræ naturæ unicus inventor, &c.*</note>

<summary>This page defines Natural Magic as the practical application of nature's hidden virtues and presents Zoroaster as a foundational figure who combined magical study with high theological truths.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Magic, Alchemy, Zoroaster, Eusebius, Philosophy of Nature, Albertus Magnus, Roger Bacon, Theology</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's exploration of the definition and legitimacy of magic and natural philosophy, following a discussion on the ancient origins of wisdom.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

...and so forth. Thus says **Zoroaster**, word for word: "God is the first, incorruptible, everlasting, unbegotten, without parts, most like himself, the guide of all good, expecting no reward, the best, the wisest, the father of right, having learned justice without teaching, perfect, wise by nature, and the only inventor of it."

So it follows that a **Magician** is nothing other than *a worshiper and interpreter of divine things* <note>original: "divinorum cultor & interpres"</note>; and the Art itself is nothing other *than the absolute perfection of Natural Philosophy* <note>original: "quam Naturalis Philosophiæ absoluta consummatio"</note>. Nevertheless, there is a mixture in all things: of good with evil, of falsehood with truth, and of corruption with purity. The good, the truth, and the purity in every category may well be embraced. For example, in the ancient practice of worshipping God by sacrifice, no man among the Elders who knew God refrained from worshipping the God of all power, nor did they condemn that kind of worship simply because the devil was similarly adored in the images of **Baal, Dagon, Astaroth, Chemosh, Jupiter, Apollo**, and the like.

Neither did the abuse of **Astrology** <note>In this period, "astrology" often referred to the scientific study of astronomy as well as the study of celestial influences.</note> frighten **Abraham** (if we believe the most ancient and religious writers) away from observing the motions and natures of the heavenly bodies. Neither can it discourage <note>original: "dehort"</note> wise and learned men in these days from attributing those virtues, influences, and inclinations to the stars and other lights of heaven which God has given to those, his glorious creatures.

I must expect some slanders and criticisms <note>original: "calumnies and obtrectations"</note> against this work from malicious, prejudiced men and the lazy defenders of ignorance, with whom this age swarms; but the voice and sound of the **Snake** and the **Goose** are one and the same <note>The author suggests that the hissing of critics is as mindless as the sounds of animals.</note>. However, our stomachs are not so squeamish and tender now, after so...

<summary>The author defines magic as the highest form of natural philosophy and divine interpretation, arguing that the misuse of a practice by heathens does not invalidate its legitimate use by the godly.</summary>
<keywords>Zoroaster, Magician, Natural Philosophy, Astrology, Abraham, Idolatry, Divine Influence, Defense of Magic</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the author's preface, following a discussion on the historical reception of philosophy and divinity.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

We have spent a long time feeding upon solid Divinity, and we are not so easily overshadowed or startled—having been so long enlightened in God's path—that we should relapse into that childish age in which Aristotle’s *Metaphysics* was forbidden to be read by a Council in France. <note>The author refers to the Council of Paris in 1210, which banned the teaching of Aristotle's natural philosophy.</note>

*But I urge the reader to hold a charitable opinion of this work, with my Christian protestation that my purpose herein is innocent. I entreat the reader to follow the advice of* Tabaeus: "When people are arguing, consider them both to be in the wrong and guilty until the truth is known." <note>original: "Qui litigant, sint ambo in conspectu tuo mali & rei."</note> *And if there is any scandal found in this enterprise of mine, it is taken by the reader, not given by the author. I find comfort in that axiom of* Hermes Trismegistus: "He who is pious practices philosophy to the highest degree" <note>original: "Qui pius est, summe philosophatur."</note>; *and therefore I present this work without disguise, offering it to all who possess candor and impartiality. As one observer noted, there are four types of readers:* **Sponges**, *which soak up everything without distinguishing between good and bad;* **Hour-glasses**, *which receive information and pour it out just as fast;* **Bags**, *which retain only the dregs of the spices and let the wine escape; and* **Sieves**, *which retain only the best. There are some readers of this last sort, and to them I present this* **Occult Philosophy**, <term>Occult Philosophy: The study of hidden natural and spiritual forces, often associated with Agrippa.</term> *knowing that they may reap good from it. As for those who are severe against it, they must pardon my opinion that such severity proceeds from* **Self-guiltiness**; *allow me to apply the words of* Ennodius: "It is the nature of personal wickedness to suspect others of that which they deserve themselves." <note>Ennodius was a 5th-century Bishop and poet.</note> *Indeed, it is the only comfort the guilty have: to find no one else innocent. But that this work may find some acceptance among others is the desire of*

->Robert Turner.<-

<summary>Robert Turner concludes his preface by defending the study of "Occult Philosophy" against narrow-minded critics, categorizing different types of readers and asserting his own pious intentions.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Occult Philosophy, Aristotle, Hermes Trismegistus, types of readers, censorship, Christian philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page contains a commendatory poem addressed to Robert Turner, the translator of Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s work on occult philosophy.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal row of decorative printer's ornaments (fleurons) is positioned at the top of the page, divided into two groups of ten by a central colon-like mark.</image-desc>

## To his special friend Mr. Robert Turner,
## on his judicious translation of
## Cornelius *Agrippa*.

As one who has just emerged from a trance,
Amazed by strange sights, whose secret fears
Have scarcely passed, but who—doubting whether he
May trust his own eyes—remains steadfastly
Fixed on those objects; just like him I stand,
Enraptured with amazement to behold what can
By art approach the gods, and far excel
The Angels that dwell in those bright spheres.
Behold *Agrippa* mounting the lofty skies,
Talking with Gods; and then, in a moment, he pries
Into the earth’s deep cabinet; as if he were *Mercury* <note>The Roman messenger god, associated with speed, communication, and the hermetic arts.</note>,
All kinds of spirits are his willing subjects.
His book provides even more than this: but we
Blind mortals could in no way be led to see
That light without a candle: therefore, to us,
You must be both an *Agrippa* and an *Oedipus* <note>In Greek mythology, Oedipus was the solver of the riddle of the Sphinx; here, it refers to Turner as a solver of difficult mysteries.</note>.
*Agrippa* appears once again, pulled by you
Out of the ashes of antiquity.
Let squint-eyed envy pine away, while you
Wear crowns of praise on your deserving brow.

->*I.P.B., Cambridge.* <note>original: Cantabrigiæ</note><-

<summary>A commendatory poem by an author identified as I.P.B. praising Robert Turner for his translation of Cornelius Agrippa's occult writings, comparing Turner to a guide who brings ancient wisdom back to light.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Henry Cornelius Agrippa, translation, occult philosophy, Mercury, Oedipus, Cambridge, commendatory verse</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as a commendatory poem, a common feature in 17th-century books where a friend or colleague praises the author's work to establish its credibility and value.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal line of ornamental woodcut printer's ornaments, resembling stylized fleur-de-lis or floral motifs, spans the top of the page.</image-desc>

## To his ingenious friend Mr. Turner,
## upon his Translation.

*Thrice-noble Soul! renowned epitome*
*Of learning and occult philosophy;* <note>original: "Occult Philosophie"; refers to the study of hidden or supernatural powers in nature.</note>
*Who imparts that unknown geomancy,* <note>original: "Geomancie"; a method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil or rocks.</note>
*With profound secrets of that obscure art!*
*To explain natural magic is your task;* <note>original: "Natural Magick"; in this period, this referred to the scientific study of the hidden properties of plants, minerals, and stars, distinct from "black magic."</note>
*Not to unmask hell-born necromancy;* <note>original: "Necromancie"; the practice of communicating with the dead, often associated with black magic.</note>
*Exposing mysteries to public view,*
*That previously were known to very few.*
*You do not keep your knowledge to yourself,*
*(As base, covetous misers do their wealth;* <note>original: "pelf"; a derogatory term for money or wealth, especially when gained dishonestly.</note>
*Whose numerous bags of rust-eaten gold*
*Profit no one, until they themselves are laid in the earth)*
*But, being studious of the public good, you make*
*Everyone partake of the fruits of your labors.*
*Therefore, if some fault-finding critic blames*
*Your writings, surely then his judgment is flawed.*
*Art has no hater but an empty head,*
*Which can far better complain than imitate.*
*Indeed, Zoilus or Momus will not dare* <note>Zoilus and Momus were figures from Greek mythology and history used as personifications of harsh, unfair, or cynical criticism.</note>
*To blame your translation, which is beyond compare*
*Excellent. So that if a hundred tongues*
*Mother Nature had bestowed, and lungs of brass,*
*Yet to rightly proclaim your praises,* <note>original: "ebuccinate"; from the Latin "ebuccinare," meaning to trumpet forth or proclaim loudly.</note>
*I would lack strength, as well as polished phrases.*
*But if the Gods will grant what I do crave,*
*Then Enoch’s translation shall you have.* <note>A clever pun on the word "translation." In the Bible, Enoch was "translated" (taken directly) to heaven without dying; the poet hopes the author receives a similar divine reward for his literary translation.</note>

->W.P., St. John's College, Cambridge<-

## To his friend the *Author*, on this his *Translation*.

What, is there not a *Sibyl* <note>A female prophet of the ancient world.</note> or *Cassandra* <note>A Trojan priestess cursed to utter true prophecies that were never believed.</note> left?
Has *Apollo* <note>The Greek and Roman god of prophecy and the arts.</note> ceased? Has sharp-fanged Time bereft
Us of the *Oracles*? Is *Dodona's* grove <note>original: "Dodan's"; an ancient oracle of Zeus where the rustling of oak leaves was interpreted as divine messages.</note>
Cut down? Does never a word proceed from *Jove* <note>Jupiter/Zeus, the king of the gods.</note>
Into the ears of mortals who inherit
*Tiresias'* soul <note>A blind prophet of Thebes.</note>, or the great *Calchas'* spirit <note>A powerful seer during the Trojan War.</note>?
What has become of the *Augurs* <note>Ancient Roman religious officials who observed natural signs, especially the flight of birds, to interpret the divine will.</note> who foretold
Nature's intents? Are the *Magi* <note>Wise men or magicians, originally Zoroastrian priests.</note> dead, who could
Tell what was done in every *Sphere* <note>In the Ptolemaic system, the celestial shells surrounding the Earth.</note>? Shall we
Not know what is done in the remotest country
Without great travel? Can we not here below descry
The mind of the gods above? All this is achieved by you,
*Agrippa* <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), the author of the original Latin text on occult philosophy.</note>; all their arts lie hidden within you.
The *Art* that before in diverse heads did lie,
Is now collected into one monopoly.
But all was in vain; we lacked an *Oedipus* <note>The mythical Greek figure who solved the riddle of the Sphinx; here used to mean a brilliant interpreter.</note>,
Who should interpret the meaning for us:
This you accomplish with such dexterity,
Adding perhaps what the *Author* never did see;
So that we may say, you do the *Art* renew:
To you the greater half of the praise is due.

->*J. B. Cambridge.* <note>original: "Cantabrigiæ"; the Latin name for Cambridge.</note><-

## To the Author, on his Translation of Cornelius Agrippa.

You are the Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom and war</note> of Learning, if named a Goddess;
Your knowledge has explained that original model;
Which Nature, also striving to combine
Science and Learning in this form of yours,
Does not show to us darkly, but clearly reveals
The knowledge of mysteries as if enshrined in you.
It is by your permission that we have access
Into Geomancy <note>original: "Geomancy"; a method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand</note>; which, unless
You had unmasked it, would have remained a mystery,
A task too hard for mortals to explain.
Since you have preserved this from the Lethean floods <note>original: "Lethæan floods"; referring to Lethe, the river of forgetfulness in the underworld, implying the translator saved this knowledge from being forgotten</note>,
We will consecrate the laurel buds
To you: (dismissing Phoebus <note>Apollo, the god of prophecy and the sun</note>) yours shall be
The Oracle to which all people shall flee
In times of danger; your predictions shall
Enthrall our willing hearts to whatever
You command; yes, you shall be
Our sole Prophet, and we shall be obedient to you.
->J. R.<-

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut border identical to the one at the top of the page, composed of repeating stylized floral fleurons.</image-desc>

## To the Author, on this his ingenious Translation of Cornelius Agrippa.

What is it I see? Agrippa made to wear
An English habit <note>original: "habit"; meaning clothing or style</note>? Surely this is something rare.
Or are his Roman <note>original: "Romane"; referring to the Latin language in which Agrippa originally wrote</note> garments, by your wit,
Translated into an English style so fit
To illustrate him? For we see that you have
Enlightened his obscure philosophy;
And that which remained so intricate,
You have exposed to every common <note>original: "vulgar"; meaning the general public or those who do not read Latin</note> brain.
If your beams shine so clearly through such dark works,
How splendid will they appear in your own!
Then go on, brave soul, to spread such rays
Of learning through the world as may speak your praise,
And fear no critics: for you, by a spell,
Can force their tongues to stay behind their teeth.
->John Tabor, of St. John’s College in Cambridge.<-

## *To the Author, on his Translation of* Cornelius Agrippa.

Does Apollo <note>original: "Phœbus"</note> *cease to answer to our demands?*
*Or will he not accept from mortals' hands*
*A sacrifice for a place struck by lightning?* <note>original: "Bidental" — a Roman sacrifice performed on a spot struck by lightning, which was then considered sacred and forbidden to tread upon.</note> *And is the* Sibyl's *cave*
*Uninhabitable? Or may* Tiresias <note>A famous blind prophet of Apollo in Thebes.</note> *have*
*No successor nor rival? How shall we*
*Then direct* Oedipus *to the world? If he*
*Adds incest to parricide, they are dumb*
*Who could predict what things should surely come;*
*And they are silent who knew when to apply*
*Purging and bloodletting* <note>original: "Phlebotomy"</note> *to our body-politic.*
*How will bold thieves rob our treasures? Who shall*
*Regain lost goods, or by his charms recall*
*The guilty?* <note>original: "nocent"</note> *The Art is by you reprieved;*
*In you the* Magi <note>Ancient Persian priests or magicians; the root of the word "magic."</note> *seem to be revived.*
Apollo *is not brain-sick, Jove’s doves are not dead,*
*The Oracles have not ceased: Agrippa’s bed*
*(Like the* Arabian *bird’s* <note>The Phoenix.</note> *self-built nest,*
*Which first proves to be her funeral urn, then her place of rebirth)*
*Has produced you, who are surely more than his equal;*
*Otherwise, this Art would have remained obscure until now—*
*A miracle to the common people* <note>original: "vulgars"</note>, *well known to none,*
*Scarcely understood by those of the deepest intellect.*
*Then I will conclude: since you explain him so well,*
*The younger brother* <note>The translator, following after the original author.</note> *has the better brain.*

->John Tomlinson,<-
->of St. John’s College in Cambridge.<-

## To his good friend the Author, on his Translation of Occult Philosophy and Geomancy.

*Most noble undertakings! It is as if Art*
*And Prudence should make a bargain to impart*
*Refulgent <note>original: "refulgent lustres"; meaning radiant or shining light</note> brilliance: you send forth a ray*
*Which even the noblest Patrons never could display.*
*Well may Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the moon and hunting, often associated with hidden knowledge and the night</note> love you, and inspire*
*Your noblest Genius with celestial fire,*
*Whose sparkling Fancy with more power can quell,*
*And sooner conquer than a Magic Spell.*
*The Author <note>Referring to Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, the original author of "Occult Philosophy"</note> thought not, (when he penned the book)*
*To be surpassed by a higher look,*
*Or be overtopped by a more triumphant strain,*
*Which should exalt his then-most pleasant style.*
*But seeing that a later descendant*
*Has snatched his honor from obscurity,*
*Both shall revive, and make spectators know*
*The best deservers of the Laurel <note>A symbol of victory and poetic excellence</note> bow.*
*Nature and Art here strive to get the victory:*
*And though he refuses to yield,*
*You have gotten the start: though he triumphs in praise,*
*Yet may his Ivy <note>A plant associated with poets, but often ranked below the laurel</note> wait upon your Bays <note>Laurel wreaths given to honor great achievements</note>.*

->M. S.<-
->At Cambridge. <note>original: "Cantabrigiæ"</note><-

Henry Cornelius Agrippa

And these eight figures create eight Houses of Heaven <note>In astrology and geomancy, "Houses" represent different areas of life, such as wealth, health, or travel</note> in this manner, by placing the figures from the left hand toward the right: as the four **Mothers** <note>original: *Matres*</note> make the first four Houses, so the four **Daughters** <note>original: *Filiæ*</note> make the four following Houses, which are the fifth, sixth, seventh, and eighth. The rest of the Houses are found in this way: that is to say, out of the first and second is derived the ninth; out of the third and fourth, the tenth; out of the fifth and sixth, the eleventh; and out of the seventh and eighth, the twelfth. This is done by the combination or joining together of two figures according to the rule of the even or odd <note>original: uneven</note> number in the remaining points of each figure. 

In the same way, there are produced out of the last four figures—that is to say, from the ninth, tenth, eleventh, and twelfth—two figures which they call **Co-adjutors** <note>original: *Coadjutrices*</note>, or **Witnesses** <note>original: *Testes*</note>. From these two, one final figure is established, which is called the **Index** <note>Also known as the Judge</note> of the whole figure, or the matter being questioned <note>original: Quesited</note>; as appears in the following example.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for geomantic divination, shifting from the "common" method used by most practitioners to a more advanced system that integrates geomancy with astrological principles.</meta>

# Of Geomancy.

What we have already explained is the common method used by geomancers, which we neither completely reject nor fully praise. However, this must also be considered in our interpretations. Now, I shall provide you with the true chart of geomancy, established according to the proper principles of astrological logic, which is as follows.

As the previously mentioned **Mothers** <note>original: *Matres*</note> create the four angles of a chart, the first Mother makes the first angle, the second Mother the second angle, the third Mother the third angle, and the fourth Mother the fourth angle. Similarly, the four **Daughters** <note>original: *Filiæ*</note> arising from the Mothers establish the four <term>succedent houses</term> <note>Houses that "succeed" or follow the angular houses (the 2nd, 5th, 8th, and 11th)</note>. The first Daughter makes the second house, the second Daughter the eleventh, the third Daughter the eighth, and the fourth Daughter makes the first house. The rest of the houses, which are <term>Cadents</term> <note>Houses that "fall away" from the angles (the 3rd, 6th, 9th, and 12th)</note>, are to be calculated according to the rule of their <term>Triplicity</term> <note>A grouping of three signs or houses</note>. That is to say: the ninth house is made from the fourth and fifth; the sixth is made from the tenth and second; the third is made from the seventh and eleventh; and the twelfth is made from the fourth and eighth.

Now you have the entire chart for a true judgment, established according to accurate and effective logic, through which I shall show you how to complete it. The figure that appears in the first house will give you the <term>sign ascending</term> <note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon</note>, which the first figure represents. Once this is done, you shall assign the zodiac signs to the rest of the houses according to their standard order. Then, in every house, you shall note the <term>Planets</term> according to the nature of the figure found there. 

From all of these components, you shall build your interpretation according to the meaning of the planets in the signs and houses where they are found, as well as their <term>aspects</term> <note>The geometric angles between planets that determine their relationship</note> to one another, and their relationship to the <term>querent</term> <note>The person asking the question</note> and the <term>quesited</term> <note>The person or thing being asked about</note>. You shall judge according to the nature of the signs ascending in their houses, the characteristics and properties of the figures placed in the various houses, and the influence of other figures forming aspects with them. This is how the "Index" of the chart—which most geomancers create—is found within the previously described figure.

But here we shall give you the secret of the entire art, to 

find <note>catchword: finde</note>

<summary>This page explains an advanced geomantic method that maps the primary figures (Mothers and Daughters) to specific astrological houses to create a more detailed chart for interpretation. It emphasizes using astrological signs, planetary associations, and aspects to refine the accuracy of a divination.</summary>
<keywords>geomancy, astrological houses, Mothers, Daughters, angular houses, succedent houses, cadent houses, triplicity, ascending sign, aspects, querent, quesited</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for geomantic divination, explaining how to calculate the "Index" to resolve ambiguous readings. It also introduces the next section of the text: the classification of questions by astrological house.</meta>

Find the Index in the following figure, which is done like this: count all the points contained in the lines of the projections and divide this total by twelve. Starting from the <term>Ascendant</term> <note>The first house of the chart, representing the beginning</note>, count through the various houses based on the remainder. The house where the count ends provides a reliable judgment of the matter <term>quesited</term> <note>original: "queſited"; the subject or person being asked about in a divination</note>; this is used together with the interpretations mentioned previously. However, if the results are equal or ambiguous on either side, then the <term>Index</term> <note>In geomancy, a specific figure used as a final tie-breaker or summary of the reading</note> alone will inform you about the matter you are asking about. An example of this figure is placed here.

<image-desc>A geomantic astrological square chart used to determine an "Index." The diagram consists of an outer square divided into twelve triangular and diamond-shaped sections (Houses) surrounding a smaller central square. Symbols for the twelve signs of the Zodiac (Aries ♈, Taurus ♉, Gemini ♊, Cancer ♋, Leo ♌, Virgo ♍, Libra ♎, Scorpio ♏, Sagittarius ♐, Capricorn ♑, Aquarius ♒, Pisces ♓) are printed around the perimeter of the outer square. Inside each house is a geomantic figure composed of dots and a corresponding planetary or lunar node symbol (including ♀ Venus, ♄ Saturn, ♂ Mars, ☋ South Node/Cauda Draconis, ☽ Moon, and ♃ Jupiter). The central square contains the following italicized text: "There remains a remainder of 6 points from the division of the projections; therefore, Jupiter in the sixth house shows the Index."</image-desc>

It now remains for us to explain which house corresponds to which type of question, and what each figure indicates or signifies regarding all questions in every house.

<meta>This page continues from the previous explanation of the Index, moving into a detailed breakdown of the twelve "Houses" of geomancy, which correspond to the astrological houses used to categorize different areas of life.</meta>

# Of Geomancy.

First, therefore, we shall address the meanings of the Houses, which are as follows:

The first House shows the person of the <term>Querent</term> <note>The person asking the question.</note>, whenever a <term>Question</term> <note>The specific inquiry or topic being divined.</note> is proposed concerning themselves, their own affairs, or anything belonging to them. This House provides the judgment on the life, physical form, state, condition, habits, temperament, appearance, and the complexion of men. 

The second House contains the judgment of possessions, riches, poverty, gain and loss, good and bad fortune, and accidental matters regarding property, such as theft, loss, or negligence. 

The third House signifies brothers, sisters, and <term>collateral relatives</term> <note>original: "Collaterals in blood"; family members who are not in a direct line of descent, like cousins.</note>. It judges short journeys and the loyalty of men. 

The fourth House signifies fathers and grandfathers, heritage and inheritance, possessions, buildings, fields, treasure, and hidden things. It also provides a description of those who lack anything due to theft, loss, or negligence. 

The fifth House gives judgment on <term>legates</term> <note>original: "Legats"; ambassadors or official messengers.</note>, messengers, rumors, and news; honors and events occurring after death; and questions that may be asked concerning pregnant women or animals. 

The sixth House gives judgment on illnesses and medicines; household members and servants; and livestock and domestic animals. 

The seventh House signifies marriage, adultery, and sexual misconduct; it renders judgment on friends, conflicts, and controversies; and matters handled before judges. 

The eighth House signifies death and things acquired through death, such as legacies and <term>hereditaments</term> <note>Inherited property or assets.</note>, as well as a wife’s dowry or marriage portion. 

The ninth House shows long journeys, faith, and constancy; dreams, the divine sciences, and religion. 

The tenth House signifies honors and <term>magisterial offices</term> <note>High-ranking government, administrative, or judicial positions.</note>. 

The eleventh House signifies friends and the wealth of princes. 

The twelfth House signifies enemies, servitude, imprisonment, and misfortune—and whatever evil can happen besides death and sickness (the judgments for which are found in the sixth and eighth houses).

It now remains for us to show what each of the figures mentioned earlier signifies in these positions, which we will now explain.

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

# Henry Cornelius Agrippa

<margin>The Greater Fortune.</margin> **The Greater Fortune** <note>original: "Fortuna major"</note> being found in the first House, gives long life and frees one from the trouble of diseases. It shows a man to be noble, magnanimous, of good manners, of average height, with a reddish complexion, curling hair, and his upper limbs larger than his lower ones. 

In the second House, it signifies clear wealth and clear gain, good fortune, and the finding of anything lost or mislaid; the capturing of a thief, and the recovery of stolen things. 

In the third House, it signifies brothers and relatives, nobles, and people of good conduct <note>original: "converſation"; in this context, it refers to one's social behavior and way of life.</note>; journeys will be prosperous and profitable with honor. It shows men to be faithful and their friendship to be sincere. 

In the fourth House, it represents a father to be noble, of good reputation, and known by many people. It expands possessions in cities, increases inheritances <note>original: "Patrimonies"</note>, and discovers hidden treasures. In this place, it also signifies theft and recovers everything lost. 

In the fifth House, it gives joy through children and causes them to achieve great honors. It makes embassies <note>original: "Embaſſages"; diplomatic missions or official messages sent via a messenger.</note> successful, though they are obtained with effort and requests. It marks rumors as being true; it bestows public honors and causes a man to be very famous after death. It predicts that a pregnant woman will give birth to a boy. 

In the sixth House, it frees one from diseases and shows that those who have illnesses shall recover in a short time. It signifies a doctor to be faithful and honest, and that he will administer good medicine <note>original: "Phyſick"</note>, which should not be viewed with suspicion. It shows household servants and assistants to be faithful; and among animals, it primarily signifies horses. 

In the seventh House, it gives a wife who is rich, honest, of good manners, loving, and pleasant. It overcomes conflicts and arguments. But if the question is about them, it signifies that the opponents are very powerful and have great influence. 

In the eighth House, if a question is asked about the death of anyone, it signifies that they shall live. It shows the type of death to be good and natural, with an honest burial and honorable funerals. It predicts a wife will have a rich dowry, legacies, and inheritance. 

In the ninth House, it signifies journeys to be prosperous—and by land on horseback rather than on foot—to be

...long, and not quickly completed. It shows the return of those who are absent and signifies that men will be of good faith, constant in their intentions, and religious—people who never change or alter their faith. It predicts that dreams will come true and signifies true and perfect branches of knowledge <note>original: "Sciences"</note>. In the tenth House, it predicts great honors, bestows public offices, magisterial positions, and legal authority <note>original: "Judgments"</note>, as well as honors in the courts of princes. It signifies that judges will be just and not corrupted by gifts. It brings a legal case <note>original: "Cause"</note> to be easily and quickly resolved. It shows kings to be powerful, fortunate, and victorious, and indicates that victory is certain. It signifies that a mother will be noble and live a long life. In the eleventh House, it signifies true and helpful friends; a rich and generous prince; and it makes a man fortunate and beloved by his sovereign. In the twelfth House, if a question is asked about the nature of enemies, it shows them to be powerful and noble, and very difficult to resist. However, if the question concerns any other condition or aspect regarding enemies, it will deliver one from their betrayals. It signifies faithful servants and brings back runaways <note>original: "reduceth fugitives"</note>. It represents animals such as horses, lions, and bulls. It frees people from imprisonment and either lessens or removes imminent <note>original: "eminent"</note> dangers.

<margin>The Lesser Fortune.</margin>
**The Lesser Fortune** <note>original: "Fortuna minor"</note> in the first House gives a long life, but one burdened with various troubles <note>original: "molestations"</note> and illnesses. It signifies a person of short stature and a lean body, having a mole <note>original: "mold"</note> or mark on their forehead or right eye. In the second House, it signifies wealth <note>original: "substance"</note>, but wealth that will be consumed by too much extravagance <note>original: "prodigality"</note>. It hides a thief, and a stolen item will scarcely be recovered, and then only with great effort. In the third House, it causes discord among siblings and relatives. It threatens danger during a journey, though the person will escape it. It makes men faithful but of a secretive and guarded <note>original: "close and hidden"</note> mind. In the fourth House, it damages family estates <note>original: "Patrimonies"</note> and inheritances. It conceals treasures, and lost things cannot be regained except with great difficulty. It signifies a father who is honest but who wastes his estate through extravagance, leaving small portions to his children. **The Lesser Fortune** <note>original: "Fortuna minor"</note> in the fifth House gives few children; it signifies that a pregnant woman will have a daughter <note>original: "woman-childe"</note>;

*Amissio* <note>Latin: "Loss"</note> in the first House signifies that the sick person will not live long and shows a short life. In the second House, *Amissio* consumes all wealth <note>original: "substance"</note> and makes one suffer and undergo the burden of miserable poverty. Neither the thief nor the stolen item shall be found. It signifies that treasure is not in the place being searched and that it will be sought after with loss and damage. In the third House, *Amissio* signifies the death of siblings <note>original: "brethren"</note>, or a lack of them, as well as a lack of relatives and friends. It signifies no journeys and causes one to be deceived by many. In the fourth House, *Amissio* signifies the utter destruction of one’s inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note>; it shows the father to be poor and the son to die. In the fifth House, *Amissio* shows the death of children and afflicts a person with various <note>original: "divers"</note> sorrows. It signifies that a woman is not pregnant or has suffered a miscarriage. It brings no fame or honors and spreads false rumors. In the sixth House, *Amissio* signifies that the sick person has recovered or shall soon recover, but it causes loss and damage regarding servants and livestock <note>original: "cattel"</note>. In the seventh House, *Amissio* gives an adulterous wife who opposes <note>original: "contrarying"</note> her husband with continual conflict; nevertheless, she shall not live long, and it causes disputes to end. In the eighth House, *Amissio* signifies that a person is dead. It consumes a wife’s dowry and grants or sends no inheritances or legacies. In the ninth House, *Amissio* causes no journeys except those that will be achieved <note>original: "compassed"</note> with great loss. It signifies people who are inconsistent in religion, often changing their opinion from one sect to another, and who are entirely ignorant of learning. In the tenth House, *Amissio* makes rulers <note>original: "Princes"</note> most unfortunate and shows that they shall be compelled to end their lives in exile and

In the twelfth House, *Amissio* destroys all enemies and keeps one long in prison, but it protects one from dangers.

<margin>Conjunction</margin>
*Conjunctio* <note>Latin: "Conjunction" or "Joining"</note> in the first House creates a prosperous life and signifies a man of medium height <note>original: "stature"</note>, neither lean nor fat. He has a long face, straight <note>original: "plain"</note> hair, a small beard, and long fingers and thighs; he is generous <note>original: "liberal"</note>, friendly, and a companion to many people. In the second House, *Conjunctio* does not signify that any riches will be gained, but it keeps a man secure and free from the disasters <note>original: "calamities"</note> of poverty. It exposes both the thief and the stolen item and helps in acquiring hidden treasure. In the third House, it brings various journeys with varying success and signifies good faith and reliability <note>original: "constancy"</note>. In the fourth House, *Conjunctio* shows a modest <note>original: "meane"</note> inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note>. It causes a father to be honest, to have a good reputation <note>original: "report"</note>, and to be of good intelligence. In the fifth House, it gives children of clever <note>original: "subtile"</note> ingenuity and wit, shows a pregnant woman will have a boy, and raises men to honors through their own sheer intelligence and cleverness. It spreads their fame and reputation <note>original: "credit"</note> far and wide and also signifies that news and rumors are true. In the sixth House, *Conjunctio* signifies that illnesses will be tedious and long-lasting; however, it predicts that the doctor <note>original: "physician"</note> will be learned and well-experienced. It also shows that servants will be faithful and blameless, and animals will be profitable. In the seventh House, it provides a wife who is very obedient, compliant <note>original: "conformable"</note>, and dutiful to her husband, possessing good wit and intelligence. It causes difficult lawsuits <note>original: "suits"</note> and controversies, as well as crafty, clever <note>original: "subtil"</note>, and malicious opponents. In the eighth House, *Conjunctio* signifies that the person about whom a question is asked is dead and suggests <note>original: "pretendeth"</note> some profit will be gained from his death. It also shows that a wife will not be very rich. In the ninth House, it brings a few journeys that are long and tiring <note>original: "tedious"</note> and shows that someone who is absent shall return after a long time. In this House, *Conjunctio* increases various arts,

<margin>White</margin>
*Albus* <note>Latin: "White"</note> in the first House signifies a life troubled with continual sickness and grievous diseases; it signifies a man of short stature, a broad chest, and thick arms, having curled or frizzled hair; one with a wide, full mouth, a great talker and babbler, much given to useless and unprofitable conversation; yet one who is merry, joyful, and cheerful, and very pleasing to others. In the second House, *Albus* expands and increases wealth <note>original: "substance"</note> gained through sports, plays, and low <note>original: "vile and base"</note> arts and exercises, but those that are pleasing and delightful; such as by plays, pastimes, dancing, and laughter. It reveals both the thief and the theft (or the thing stolen) and hides and conceals treasure. In the third House, *Albus* signifies very few siblings <note>original: "brethren"</note>; it gives not many, but tedious and wearying journeys, and signifies all kinds of deceivers. In the fourth House, it shows very little or no inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note>, and a father who is a well-known man; but it declares him to be a man of some low and inferior office and employment. In the fifth House, *Albus* gives no children, or if any, they shall soon die; it declares a woman to be servile, and causes those who are pregnant <note>original: "with young"</note> to miscarry, or else to give birth to deformed children <note>original: "monsters"</note>; it denotes all rumors to be false, and leads to no honor. <margin>The Boy</margin> *Puer* <note>Latin: "The Boy"</note> in the first House gives a moderately <note>original: "indifferent"</note> long life, but a laborious one; it raises men to great fame through military rank <note>original: "dignity"</note>; and signifies a person with a strong body, a ruddy complexion, a handsome face, and black hair. In the second House, *Puer* increases wealth <note>original: "substance"</note> obtained through other people's goods—by plundering, robbery <note>original: "rapines"</note>, confiscations, military laws, and the like; it conceals both the thief and the stolen item, but discovers no treasure. In the third House, *Puer* raises a man to honor above his brothers and to be feared by them; it signifies that journeys will be dangerous and indicates people of good reputation. In the fourth House, *Puer* signifies doubtful inheritances and possessions, and signifies that a father attained his wealth and estate through violence. In the fifth House, *Puer* shows good children and those who shall attain honors and high offices <note>original: "dignities"</note>; it signifies that a woman will have a male child and shows that honors are to be acquired.

In the sixth House, *Puer* <note>Latin: "The Boy"</note> causes violent diseases and infirmities, such as wounds, falls, contusions, and bruises; but he easily delivers the sick, and shows the physician and surgeon <note>original: "Chirurgion"</note> to be good. In the seventh House, *Puer* causes a wife to be a virago <term>virago</term> <note>a woman of masculine spirit, strength, and courage</note> of a bold spirit and of good fidelity, and one who loves to rule and govern a house. He creates cruel strifes and contentions, and such adversaries as shall hardly be restrained by justice. 

*Puer* in the eighth House shows him who is supposed to be dead to be alive; he signifies that the kind of death will not be painful or difficult, but will proceed from some hot humor <term>humor</term> <note>In traditional medicine, a fluid in the body—such as blood or bile—that was thought to determine health and temperament; "hot" humors were associated with fevers or inflammation</note>, or by iron, or the sword, or some other cause of a similar kind. He shows a man to have no legacies or other inheritance. 

In the ninth House, *Puer* shows that journeys are not to be undertaken without peril and danger of life, yet nevertheless declares them to be accomplished successfully and safely. He shows persons of little religion and using little conscience; notwithstanding, he gives the knowledge of natural philosophy and medicine <note>original: "physick"</note>, and many other liberal and excellent arts. 

*Puer* in the tenth House signifies princes to be powerful, glorious, and famous in warlike achievements; but they shall be fickle and changeable <note>original: "unconstant and unchangeable" — likely a printer's error for "changeable"</note> by reason of the shifting and various success of victory. *Puer* in this House causes judges to be cruel and unmerciful; he increases offices in military affairs and signifies that the magistracy is exercised by fire and sword. He hurts a mother and endangers her life. 

In the eleventh House, *Puer* shows noble friends and noblemen, and those who shall frequently attend the courts of princes and follow after warfare. He causes many to stick by cruel men; nevertheless, he causes much esteem with princes, though their favor is to be suspected. 

*Puer* in the twelfth House causes enemies to be cruel and destructive <note>original: "pernicious"</note>; those who are in prison shall escape, and he makes them avoid many dangers.

In the second House, *Rubeus* <note>Latin: "The Red"</note> signifies poverty and creates thieves and robbers, as well as such persons as shall seek after their maintenance and livelihoods by using false, wicked, evil, and unlawful arts. It protects thieves and conceals theft, and signifies that no treasure is to be hidden or found. 

In the third House, *Rubeus* renders brothers and kinsmen full of hatred and odious to one another; it shows them to be of evil manners and ill disposition. It causes journeys to be very dangerous and foreshows false faith and treachery. 

In the fourth House, he destroys and consumes patrimonies <term>patrimonies</term> <note>inherited estates or property passed down from a father</note>; he disperses and wastes inheritances, causing them to come to nothing. *Rubeus* in the fifth House gives many children, but they shall either be wicked and disobedient, or else shall afflict their parents with grief, disgrace, and infamy. 

In the sixth House, *Rubeus* causes mortal wounds, sicknesses, and diseases; he who is sick shall die. The physician shall err <note>the doctor will make a mistake in treatment or diagnosis</note>, servants will prove false and treacherous, and cattle and beasts shall cause hurt and danger. 

In the seventh House, *Rubeus* signifies a wife to be infamous, publicly adulterous, and contentious. It signifies deceitful and treacherous adversaries who shall endeavor to overcome you by crafty and subtle wiles and circumventions <term>circumventions</term> <note>legal tricks or maneuvers used to get around the law</note> of the law. 

In the eighth House, *Rubeus* signifies a violent death to be inflicted by the execution of public justice. If any missing person is inquired after, it signifies that he is certainly dead, and it signifies that a wife shall have no portion or dowry. 

*Rubeus* in the ninth House shows journeys to be evil and dangerous, and that a man shall be in danger of being either spoiled by thieves and robbers or taken by plunderers. It declares men to be of most wicked opinions in religion and of evil faith, and such as will easily be induced to deny and abandon their faith for every small occasion. It denotes sciences to be false and deceitful, and their professors to be ignorant.

<margin>Prison <note>Latin: "Carcer"</note></margin>
When *Carcer* <note>Latin: "The Prison"</note> is positioned in the first house, it gives a short life; it signifies men to be mostly wicked, of a filthy, cruel, and unclean figure and shape, and such as are hated and despised by all men. 

*Carcer* in the second house causes most cruel and miserable poverty; it signifies both that the thief and the stolen thing shall be captured and regained; and it shows no treasure to be hidden. 

In the third house, *Carcer* signifies hatred and dissension among brothers, evil journeys, and most wicked faith and conduct. 

*Carcer* in the fourth house signifies a man to have no possessions or inheritances, a father to be most wicked, and to die a sudden and evil death. 

In the fifth house, *Carcer* gives many children; it shows a woman not to be with child <note>i.e., not currently pregnant</note>, and provokes those who are pregnant to miscarry by their own consent, or kills the child. It signifies no honors and disperses most false rumors. 

In the sixth house, *Carcer* causes the diseased to undergo long sickness; it signifies servants to be wicked or rather unprofitable, and physicians to be ignorant. 

In the seventh house, *Carcer* shows that the wife shall be hated by her husband, and signifies that lawsuits and contentions will be badly ended and decided.

<margin>Sorrow <note>Latin: "Tristitia"</note></margin>
*Tristitia* <note>Latin: "Sorrow" or "Sadness"</note> in the first house does not shorten life, but afflicts it with many troubles <term>molestations</term>. Treasure shall not be found, nor shall the thief or the stolen goods. *Tristitia* in the third house signifies that a man will have few brothers <note>or siblings</note>, but shows that he shall outlive them all; it causes unhappy journeys but gives good faith <note>honesty or reliability</note>. <sig>F 2.</sig>

<note>This is a continuation of the Dragon's Head in the 7th house.</note> signifies that a man shall have many wives and multiplies and stirs up many adversaries and lawsuits. In the eighth house, it shows death to be certain, increases legacies and inheritances, and gives a good portion <note>a dowry or inheritance</note> with a wife. In the ninth house, **The Dragon’s Head** <note>original: "Caput Draconis"</note> signifies many journeys, many sciences, and good religion; it shows that those who are absent shall soon return. In the tenth house, it signifies glorious princes, great and magnificent judges, great offices, and profitable magistracy <note>a position as a civil officer or judge</note>. In the eleventh house, it causes one to have many friends and to be beloved by all men. In the twelfth house, **The Dragon’s Head** signifies that men will have many enemies and many women; it detains the imprisoned and punishes them severely.

<margin>The Dragon's Tail.</margin>
**The Dragon’s Tail** <note>original: "Cauda Draconis"</note>, in each and every one of the relevant houses mentioned before, gives the opposite judgment to the Dragon's Head. And these are the natures of the figures of Geomancy and their judgments in each and every one of their houses, regarding all manner of questions to be proposed concerning any matter or thing whatsoever.

But now, regarding the manner of proceeding to judgment, you must especially observe this: that whenever any question is proposed to you which is contained in any of the houses, you shall not only answer it by the figure contained in that specific house; but by beholding and diligently respecting all the figures, and the **Index** <note>a specific calculation or figure used to summarize the chart</note> itself in two houses, you shall establish the basis of your judgment. You shall, therefore, consider the figure of the thing sought <note>original: "quesited"</note> or inquired after; if it should appear again <note>original: "multiply himself"</note> in other places of the figure, you may cause those places also to be partakers in your judgment. 

For example, if a question is proposed regarding the second house concerning a thief, and the figure of the second house is also found in the sixth house, it declares the thief to be someone from one's own household or a servant <note>the 2nd house governs possessions, while the 6th governs servants</note>. In this manner you shall judge and consider the rest; for this whole Art consists in the mixtures <note>original: "Commixtures"</note> of the figures and their natures. Whoever practices this rightly shall always declare the most true and certain judgments upon every particular thing whatsoever.

<summary>This page concludes the house-by-house meanings for the geomantic figure "The Dragon's Head," notes that "The Dragon's Tail" has the opposite effects, and explains that accurate divination requires analyzing how figures repeat across different houses.</summary>

<keywords>Geomancy, Caput Draconis, Cauda Draconis, Dragon's Head, Dragon's Tail, astrological houses, divination methods, interpretation rules</keywords>

IN our books of **Occult Philosophy** <note>This refers to Agrippa's influential Three Books of Occult Philosophy, which categorized magic into Natural, Celestial, and Ceremonial types.</note>, we have not explained the principles, foundations, and reasons of magic itself so concisely as we have extensively. We have shown how its experiments should be selected, chosen, and combined to produce many wonderful effects. However, in those books, these subjects are treated theoretically rather than practically. Additionally, some topics are not handled completely or fully, and others are presented very figuratively, as if they were enigmas <note>original: "Ænigmatically"</note> or obscure riddles. These are subjects we have mastered through great study, diligence, and very meticulous investigation, though they were previously set forth in a more unrefined and undeveloped manner. serving as a completion and key to our other books of *Occult Philosophy* and all magical operations, we will provide you with the teachings of holy and pure truth, unconquerable and irresistible magical discipline, and the most delightful experiments of the holy deities. This, therefore, is to be known: that the names of the presiding intelligences <term>intelligent presidents: In Renaissance magic, 'intelligences' were considered the benevolent spiritual entities governing the planets.</term> of every one of the planets are formed in this manner: that is to say, by collecting the letters out of the astrological chart <note>original: "figure of the world"</note>, starting from the rising of the body of the planet, according to the order of the signs through the various degrees; and out of those degrees, from the aspects of the planet itself, the calculation being made from the degree of the ascendant <note>The "ascendant" is the degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment.</note>. In the same manner are formed the names of the princes of the malevolent spirits; they are taken under all the planets of the presiding spirits in a reverse <note>original: "retrograde"</note> order, the calculation being made contrary to the order of the signs, starting from the beginning of the Seventh House <note>The Seventh House represents the western horizon or the Descendant.</note>. 

Now, the name of the supreme and highest intelligence, which many suppose to be the "Soul of the World" <note>original Latin: *Anima Mundi*—the divine essence that permeates all nature.</note>, is collected out of the four cardinal points of the astrological chart, according to the method already described. Conversely, the name of the great *Daemon* <note>original Greek: *Daimon*—here used specifically to mean a malevolent spirit.</note>, or evil spirit, is known through the four cadent houses <term>cadent houses: Astrological houses (the 3rd, 6th, 9th, and 12th) that "fall away" from the main angles of a chart.</term>. In the same way, you shall understand the names of the great presiding spirits ruling in the air from the four angles of the succedent houses <term>succedent houses: Astrological houses (the 2nd, 5th, 8th, and 11th) that "follow" the main angles.</term>. To obtain the names of the good spirits, the calculation is to be made according to the order of the signs, beginning from the degree of the ascendant; to obtain the names of the evil spirits, you must work in the opposite direction.

Indeed, he is the only author whose works survive who has discussed how to obtain the names of spirits.

Now, this art is perfected and accomplished in this way: first, we must take care in placing the vowels of the letters, which are found by calculating the astrological chart <note>original: "celestial figure"</note>, to find the names of the spirits of the second order, the Presidents and Governors. 

For good spirits, this is achieved by considering the stars that constitute the letters and placing them according to their order. First, subtract the degree of the eleventh house from the degree of the star that comes first in the sequence. Whatever remains from that calculation should be projected <note>In astrology, "projecting" means counting a specific distance from a starting point along the zodiac signs.</note> from the degree of the ascendant <note>The degree of the zodiac rising over the eastern horizon at a specific moment.</note>. Where that number ends, you find the position <note>original: "part"</note> of the vowel for the first letter. Begin, therefore, to calculate the vowels of these letters according to their number and order; the vowel that falls on the position of the first star is the vowel attributed to the first letter. 

Next, you shall find the position of the second letter by subtracting the degree of the second star in the sequence from the first star; then, project the remainder from the ascendant. This is the point from which you will begin calculating the vowels; the vowel that falls upon the second star is the vowel of the second letter. You may continue to find the vowels of the subsequent letters in this way: always subtract the degree of the next star from the degree of the star immediately preceding it. Furthermore, all calculations and numbering for the names of good spirits should be done following the natural order <note>original: "succession"</note> of the zodiac signs. 

When calculating the names of evil spirits, instead of using the degree of the eleventh house (as with good spirits), you should use the degree of the twelfth house. All numbering and calculations for them should be made following the succession of the signs, but starting the count from the degree of the tenth house.

However, when extracting names using tables, the vowels are placed in a different manner. First, determine the total number of letters that make up the name itself. This is then counted from the beginning of the column for the first letter,

The value <note>original: "number"</note> of that distance is projected <note>In this context, "projected" means counting a specific distance from a starting point along a sequence or table.</note> from the beginning of its column; where it ends, there is the position <note>original: "part"</note> of the first vowel. From there, you shall calculate the vowels themselves in their own number and order within the same column. The vowel that falls upon the first letter of a name shall be assigned to that name. 

You shall find the subsequent vowels by measuring the distance from the preceding vowel to the next, following the sequence of the alphabet. The value of that distance is to be counted from the beginning of its own column; where it stops, that is the position of the vowel you are seeking. From there, you must calculate the vowels as stated above; those vowels that fall upon their corresponding letters are to be assigned to them. If any vowel happens to fall upon another vowel, the first must give way to the second. Note that this applies only to good spirits. 

For evil spirits, you may proceed in the same way, except you must perform the calculations in a reverse and backward order—contrary to the sequence of the alphabet and contrary to the order of the columns (that is to say, in ascending order).

<margin>430 to 434</margin>The method for finding the names of good angels and of every person, which we taught in our third book of *Occult Philosophy*, is based on significant authority and a solid foundation <note>original: "no little authority, nor of a mean foundation"</note>. However, we will now provide you with other methods supported by sound reasoning. 

One method involves taking the five places of the <term>Hylech</term> <note>The Hylech, or "Giver of Life," is a specific point or planet in a birth chart used by ancient astrologers to determine longevity and vital force.</note> from the birth chart <note>original: "figure of the nativity"</note>. Once these are noted, the characters of the letters are projected in their order and number starting from the beginning of <term>Aries</term> <note>The first sign of the zodiac, representing the start of the astrological year.</note>. The letters that fall upon the degrees of these places—arranged and viewed <note>original: "aspected," referring to astrological aspects, which are specific geometric angles between planets that astrologers believe change the planets' influence.</note> according to their order and dignity—form the name of an angel.

...governing stars over the five locations mentioned earlier. The calculation <note>original: "projection"; in this context, it refers to a mathematical or symbolic mapping of points across the zodiac.</note> is to be made from the degree of the ascendant. This is done by collecting the letters associated with the Almutel <note>The "Almutel" is the planet with the most combined astrological strength (essential and accidental dignity) over a specific point in the zodiac.</note>. When these letters are arranged in order according to their astrological strength <note>original: "dignity"</note>, they form the name of an Angel. 

There is furthermore another method used and highly regarded by the Egyptians. They perform their calculation from the degree of the ascendant and gather the letters according to the Almutel of the eleventh House. They call this House a good *Daemon* <note>original: "Damon"; in classical thought, a *daemon* was a guiding spirit or divine power, distinct from the modern concept of a "demon."</note>. When these letters are placed according to their astrological strengths, the names of the Angels are established. 

The names of the evil Angels are discovered in a similar way, except that the calculations must be performed in reverse—contrary to the natural order and sequence of the zodiac signs. While we calculate from the beginning of Aries when seeking the names of good spirits, we must conversely count from the beginning of Libra to find the names of the evil ones. 

Furthermore, while we count from the degree of the ascendant for good spirits, we must calculate from the degree of the seventh House for evil ones. According to the Egyptians, the name of an [evil] angel is gathered using the Almutel of the twelfth House, which they refer to as an evil spirit. 

All the rituals that we have already described in our third book of <term>Occult Philosophy</term> can be performed using the characters of any language. In all of these (as we have stated above), there is a mystical and divine number, order, and shape. Because of this, it happens that the same spirit may be called by several different names. Other names are discovered from the spirit's own name—whether good or evil—using tables created for this purpose.

These celestial symbols <note>original: "characters"; these refer to the sigils or magical marks representing spirits.</note> consist of lines and "heads." There are six types of heads, corresponding to the six magnitudes of the stars, to which the planets are also categorized. The first magnitude is represented by a star with the Sun, or a cross. The second, associated with Jupiter, is a circular point. The third, associated with Saturn, is a semicircle or a triangle—which may be curved, rounded, or pointed <note>original: "acute"</note>.

The fourth method, associated with Mars, uses a small stroke that passes through the line, whether square, straight, or diagonal <note>original: "oblique"</note>. The fifth, associated with Venus and Mercury, uses a small stroke or a dot with a tail, either pointing upward or downward. The sixth, associated with the Moon, uses a blackened dot. You may see all of these in the following table. 

When the "heads" <note>The "heads" are the primary starting points or symbolic icons used to begin the construction of a sigil.</note> are positioned according to the locations of the stars in the astrological chart <note>original: "figure of Heaven"</note>, the lines are then drawn out according to how their natures agree with one another. This is the method you should use for the fixed stars. However, when determining the characters of the planets, the lines are drawn and the "heads" are positioned according to the planets' own paths and their relationships to one another.

---

| Number and Planet | Heads | Lines joined to the Heads |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| I. $\odot$ <note>The Sun</note> | Five-pointed stars and small crosses | A horizontal line with stars and crosses at the ends and center |
| II. $\text{\jupiter}$ <note>Jupiter</note> | Small circles | A horizontal line with circles placed along its length |
| III. $\text{\mars}$ <note>Mars</note> | Angled or "V" shaped marks | A horizontal line intersected by angled strokes |
| IIII. $\text{\saturn}$ <note>Saturn</note> | A small cross and a square | A horizontal line connecting a square and a cross |
| V. $\text{\venus}$ & $\text{\mercury}$ <note>Venus and Mercury</note> | A multi-pointed star and a dot with a curved "tail" | A horizontal line connecting these two distinct symbols |
| VI. $\text{\moon}$ <note>The Moon</note> | A star and a solid black circle | A horizontal line with a six-pointed star and a blackened circle |

---

Therefore, when a character <note>original: "character"; here meaning a magical seal or sigil</note> is to be created for any celestial constellation <note>original: "Image"</note> rising in any degree or "face" of a zodiac sign, and which consists of stars of the same size <note>original: "magnitude"</note> and nature: the number of these stars should be positioned according to their place and order. Then, the lines are drawn to resemble the shape of the constellation <note>original: "Image signified"</note> as fully as possible.

<page-num>37</page-num>
