Pollux. Truly, if they have hardened themselves in wickedness, time cannot purge nor cleanse them; even if they desire it ever so much, they can never be able to accomplish it. For there is no opportunity for repentance, nor time given to them to recall that which is past.

Castor. You have now declared that the Archangel who became an apostate <note>original: "apoſtate" — one who abandons their faith or principles; here referring to Lucifer</note> drew away very many other Angels with him in his fall, so that they might become Devils. Could he not, out of his own specific malice after his fall, sufficiently rule over his own province without the Angels that fell with him?

<margin>Why the Devil has familiars.</margin> Pollux. He could. But being allured by that pride which made him so arrogantly desire the Majesty of God, he strove so far to be like God that he chose very many ministers for himself. To these, he does not commit all things he wants accomplished in general, but assigns different tasks to different spirits.

<margin>Demons of Jupiter or Midday Spirits.</margin> Those which we call *Jovii* <note>original: "Jovii" — spirits associated with Jupiter or the upper air</note> and *Antemeridiani* <note>original: "Antemeridiani" — spirits appearing before noon</note> are false Gods—that is, liars—who desire to be esteemed and adored as Gods. They are appointed as servants and slaves to the Devil, their Prince, so that they might allure the people of the earth into a common love of themselves. *Plato* says this 
> *is the fountain of all wickedness: that they may aspire to authority and greatness, covet to be gorgeously clothed, and desire to be called monarchs of the earth in perpetual power, and Gods upon earth.* 

<margin>Matthew 4.</margin> It is said that it was one of these who spoke to our Savior, showing him all the kingdoms of the earth and saying, 
> *All these things will I give you, if you will fall down and worship me.*

Castor. Certainly, these *Meridiani* <note>original: "Meridiani" — Southern or Noonday spirits</note> have shown what appears to be a madness in *Libicus Sapho* and *Diocletian* the Emperor, who accounted the utmost degree of blessedness to be reputed as Gods.

<margin>The Southern Spirits.</margin>
<margin>Libicus, Sapho, and Diocletian as Gods.</margin> Pollux. Truly, this is a certain natural foolishness of the mind and of human nature. He <note>referring to Sapho</note> began by taking certain little birds and teaching them, bit by bit, to pronounce human words and say, <gloss>Sapho is a great God</gloss> <note>original Greek: "μύσας Θεός φάρον" — likely a corruption of "μέγας θεὸς Σάπφων"</note>. When these birds could pronounce the words...

<summary>Pollux explains that the Devil organizes fallen angels into a hierarchy to mimic God's majesty, using specific demons to trick humans into pride and false worship. He cites historical figures like Diocletian and Sapho as examples of humans driven mad by the desire for divinity.</summary>

<margin>Occidental Spirits</margin>
**Pollux:** Yes, chiefly. For *Iamblichus* <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on theurgy and spirits</note> asserts that the spirits of the *water*, of the *western* <note>original: "occidental"</note> part of the world, and some *meridional* spirits are predestined to this purpose. Such are *Nisrach* and *Kellen*, who frame and contrive unlawful loves which produce shame and dishonesty, reveling and gorging, surfeiting with excessive drunkenness, wanton dances, gluttony, and vomiting. They wander about lakes, fish-ponds, and rivers; they are the worst, foulest, and most fraudulent kind of spirits. And by *Alcinach*, an occidental spirit, he causes shipwrecks, tempests...

The <term>Hebrew Astronomers</term> <note>original: "Hebrew Astronomers"; likely referring to Kabbalistic or astrological traditions</note> mentioned before say that the <term>Spirits of the Air</term> cause thunders, lightnings, and thunderbolts so that they might corrupt and infect the air, producing pestilence and destruction. **Castor:** Are there so many monsters in <term>Phlegethon</term> <note>original: "Pblegeton"; in Greek mythology, the river of fire in the underworld</note>, **Pollux**?

**Pollux:** And many more; for the same <term>Hebrew Assertors</term> declare and maintain that there are <term>Spirits of the fiery element</term> raging about like fierce <term>panthers</term>. These are active beneath the regions of the moon, and they immediately execute whatever task is committed to them.

<margin>Spirits of the earth.</margin>
And there are <term>Spirits of the earth</term> which inhabit groves, woods, and wildernesses; they are the plague and mischief of hunters. Sometimes they frequent open fields, endeavoring to seduce travelers and passengers away from their right path, or to deceive them with false and wicked illusions. Otherwise, they seek to afflict men with a harmful melancholy to make them furious or mad, so that they may hurt them and sometimes almost kill them. The leaders of these are <term>Sanyaad</term> and <term>Achimael</term>, who are oriental spirits—a kind less prone to wickedness because of the constancy of their dispositions.

<margin>Subterranean Spirits.</margin>
There are also <term>subterranean spirits</term> which inhabit dens and caverns of the earth and the remote hollows of mountains, so that they might invade deep pits and the bowels of the earth. These spirits dig up metals and guard treasures, which they often transport from one place to another to prevent any man from using them. They stir up winds with flashing flames of fire; they strike the foundations of buildings and perform frightful dances in the night, from which they suddenly vanish while making noises and the sounds of bells to cause fear in those who see them. Sometimes they act as pretenders, feigning themselves to be the souls of the dead. Nevertheless, they are ignorant in carrying out their deceits upon women; of which company...

### Of the Nature of Spirits.

Many of the Necromancers <note>original: "Negromancers"; those who claim to communicate with the dead or command spirits</note> say these are *Gazael*, *Fegor*, and *Anarazol*, the spirits of the noonday.

**Castor:** How cautiously ought a man to walk, Pollux, among so many traps and snares?

<margin>A man never walks safely.</margin>
<margin>Ephesians 6.</margin>
**Pollux:** A man never walks safely unless he fortifies and strengthens himself with the armor of God. This means his waist is belted with truth, and he wears the breastplate of righteousness; let him walk with his feet protected by the preparation of the Gospel of peace, and let him take up the shield of faith and the helmet of salvation, by which he shall smash into pieces all the arrows of his enemy. 

But hear more: besides these, there are other lying spirits (although they are all liars), yet these are more prone to lying; they are called *Pythons*, from which the god *Apollo* is called *Pythius* <note>In Greek mythology, Apollo earned this title after slaying the serpent Python; here, the author links pagan gods to demonic spirits</note>. They have a Prince, of whom mention is made in the Book of Kings, where it is said, "I will be a lying spirit in the mouth of all your prophets." 

<margin>1 Kings 11.</margin>
The spirits of iniquity differ only a little from these, and they are also called "vessels of wrath." *Belial*—whose name is interpreted to mean "one without an equal," and whom *Paul* calls an apostate or transgressor—is foully submissive to the worst inventions. *Plato* claimed such a spirit was the one who first discovered and invented stage plays and dice. To him, we will join the Monk who invented the use of gunpowder in his engines of war. 

<margin>Gunpowder.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 49.</margin>
*Jacob* makes mention of these in *Genesis* when he blesses his sons; he says, "*Simeon* and *Levi* are bloody vessels of iniquity; *O my soul, do not enter into their counsels*." 

<margin>Isaiah 7.</margin>
The *Psalmist* <note>The author of the Psalms, usually King David</note> calls these spirits "vessels of death"; *Isaiah* calls them "vessels of fury"; *Jeremiah*, "vessels of wrath"; and *Ezekiel* calls them "vessels of death and destruction." The Necromancers call the aforementioned *Belial* by the name *Chodar*, an Eastern spirit, who also has under him the spirits of jugglers <note>original: "Juglers"; in this context, street performers or illusionists suspected of using real magic</note>, who imitate and attempt to perform miracles so that they may seduce false magicians and wicked people. 

It is clearly evident that the serpent which deceived *Eve* was such a seducer, and *Satan* is his Prince, of whom it is said in the *Book of Revelation* that he would deceive the whole world. And such a spirit was the one that at *Tubinga* <note>Tübingen, Germany</note>, in the sight of many people, devoured an entire chariot and several horses.

**Castor:**

**Castor.** And what shall be the end of these false prophets and workers of wickedness? I can scarcely believe that there is any angle or corner in the whole fabric of the world that is free from them.

<margin>The world is the receptacle of devils, false accusers, and spies.</margin> **Pollux.** Scarcely the smallest mite that may be seen.

**Castor.** Therefore you truly call the world the receptacle of those false lights.

**Pollux.** If it were not most safely purged with the sword of the word of God, it would immediately be worse.

**Castor.** Without doubt.

**Pollux.** Nevertheless, I have seen many that remain whom I have not yet inscribed in this frantic catalogue.

**Castor.** Who are they?

**Pollux.** False accusers and spies, obedient to *Astaroth*, who is called a Devil <note>original: *Diabolos*</note> among the Greeks; and John <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Revelation.</note> calls him the accuser of the brethren. Also, there are tempters and deceivers that lie in wait to deceive, who are present with every man; and these we term evil angels, which have *Mammon* <note>A term used in the New Testament to personify wealth and greed.</note> for their King, and they affect men with an insatiable avarice and thirsty desire for authority and dominion. There are others

<margin>Lucifugi, flyers from the light.</margin> called <term>Lucifugi</term> <note>Latin: "light-fleers" or those who shun the light.</note>, which fly from the light, never appearing in the day, but delighting in darkness, maliciously vexing and troubling men. Sometimes, by God’s permission, they do hurt to them either by some touching, breathing, or inspiration; but truly they are a kind which are unsuited to do much wickedness because they eschew and fly from any communication with men. Pliny the Younger <note>original: *Pliny the second*</note> relates that there was such a

<margin>A horrible apparition of a spirit in the house of Athenodorus.</margin> one at Athens, in a certain spacious house which Athenodorus the philosopher happened to purchase. ## Of the Nature of Spirits.

There was a spacious house at Athens which nobody would inhabit because of the nightly incursions of spirits. These were so formidable to the inhabitants that sometimes in the daytime, even while they were awake, the spirits would cast them into a trance-like state <note>original: "cast them into dreams"</note>, so that the shapes and forms they saw were always present in their memory. 

Eventually, a certain philosopher named Athenodorus happened to purchase that house and prepared and furnished it for himself to live in. Because everyone had an evil suspicion of that house, he immediately commanded his servants to provide him with a bed and tables, so that after he had completed his studies, he might go to sleep. 

Therefore (says Pliny <note>Pliny the Younger, a Roman author who recorded this famous ghost story in his letters.</note>), when he went in during the evening and applied himself to his studies, he suddenly heard the locks shake open and the sound of chains being moved. Nevertheless, he did not lift his eyes or stir from his book, but stopped his ears with his fingers, lest that furious tumult might create a vain fear within him. 

But as the noise continued to approach nearer to him, he finally looked up and saw a ghost <note>original: "effigies"</note> like a finger beckoning and calling to him. He paid little attention to it until it had touched him three times and the noise drew nearer to the table. Then he looked up, took a light, and beheld the spirit. It appeared as an old man, wasted away with withered leanness and deformity; his beard hung down long, his hair was horrible and messy, and his legs and feet were laden with chains and fetters. The spirit went toward a gate which was bolted, and there it left the philosopher and vanished away.

**Castor.** What fearful things you relate, Pollux! But what was the outcome of this sad spectacle?

**Pollux.** The next day, he related the whole matter to the magistrates in order, just as he had seen it, advising them that they should dig diligently around the threshold of the door; for it was probable they might find something there which, if removed, might cause the house to be quiet and habitable.

**Castor.** What did they find?

**Pollux.** Having dug up the earth, Pliny says they found a dead carcass, bound and entangled in chains and fetters, with the flesh being consumed by devouring time. Without delay, they caused the body to be buried according to the proper ceremonies <note>The original text says "Christian ceremonies," though the historical Pliny was Roman; the author likely means "decent religious burial."</note>.

<summary>This page recounts Pliny the Younger's famous story of the philosopher Athenodorus, who confronted a chained ghost in a haunted Athenian house to discover its unburied remains.</summary>

**Castor:** But once this was performed, did the house afterwards become quiet and habitable?

**Pollux:** Yes, very well.

<margin>They are possessed with madness who destroy churchyards.</margin> **Castor:** What madness, therefore, possesses those who profane and destroy churchyards, where the sacred organs <note>original: "Organs," referring here to the physical bodies or remains</note> of the holy and blessed Spirit rest? They essentially give the bones of the dead as meat to the spirit *Zazelus*, who is mentioned in the third book of *Kings* <note>likely referring to 2 Kings in modern Bibles, or a specific demonological interpretation of the text</note>; and we read in *Pausanias*, among the histories of *Delphi*, that he was called *Eurynomus*.

<margin>The Spirit Zazelus and Eurynomus.</margin> **Pollux:** You shall find that the governors of cities who held the opinions and judgment of Christians actually subverted, destroyed, and profaned these holy places. They did this so that the youth might dance their mocking plays there to the furious sound of the drum or small taber, and sing, "*Hurrah, victory!*" <note>original: "Io paean," a Greek hymn of praise or triumph</note>; or, so that poor, lowly old women could sell cheap trinkets or lupine seeds <note>a common, inexpensive snack in antiquity</note> in places that God intended to be purified with holy prayers for the salvation of souls, or for the breaking of bread for the hungry.

**Castor:** But it is an impious and heathenish thing to have so touched the anointed of God.

**Pollux:** And worse than heathenish; for the heathens held the rites and ceremonies of burials in high esteem. 

<margin>The ceremony of burials was in great esteem among the heathens.</margin> *Elpenor* is a witness to this in *Homer*, where he yields up his life; in *Homer*, he speaks to *Ulysses*: "*I entreat you, O Ulysses, to be mindful of me, and not depart from here and leave me unburied, lest, by not being rightly buried, I shall bring the wrath of the Gods upon you.*" And *Archytas* the philosopher, in *Flaccus* <note>referring to the poet Horace, whose full name was Quintus Horatius Flaccus</note>, speaks thus to the mariner:

<margin>Horace, Book 1 of Odes.</margin> *The South Wind, the rapid companion of setting Orion, overwhelmed me also in the Illyrian waves. But you, sailor, do not be so stingy as to refuse to give a small portion of shifting sand to my unburied head and bones. If you do this, whatever the East Wind threatens against the Italian waves, let the Venusian woods be punished while you remain safe; and may much profit flow to you from where it rightly can.* <note>original Latin: "Me quoque devexi rapidus comes Orionis / Illyricis Notus obruit undis. / At tu nauta vagae ne parce malignus arenae / Ossibus et capiti inhumato / Particulam dare; sic quodcunque minabitur Eurus / Fluctibus Hesperiis, Venusinae / Plectantur silvae, te sospite multa que merces / Unde potest tibi defluat aequo."</note>

Orion's rapid comrade, the South Wind <note>original: "Notus"</note>, overwhelmed me
In the Illyrian Sea;
Oh seaman, do not frown or forbear to spread
Upon my bones and unburied head

Let the Hesperian waves only beat
Vainly against the Venusian woods, while you, safe and rich,
Climb to Fortune's highest pitch.
You flee; yet what may fall upon the guiltless score
Of your future sons? A similar urn,
A similar funeral, and a proud return
May wait for you too; my curses shall have force,
From which no atonements can bring relief.

And Palinurus <note>Aeneas's helmsman who died at sea</note> says to Aeneas in the sixth book of Virgil's *Aeneid*:

<margin>Virgil, *Aeneid*, Book 6.</margin>

Now the waves hold me, and the winds toss me against the shore;
By Heaven’s rejoicing light I implore you,
And by the air, by old Anchises <note>Aeneas's father</note> too,
And by the hopes of young Iulus <note>Aeneas's son; original: "Julius"</note>, you
Unconquered Hero, help me! Take me away
From these sad troubles, or lay my body
In the earth; for you have the power to do it.

<margin>The vain religion of the Pagans.</margin>
**Castor.** Have the Pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note> so greatly esteemed the ceremony of burials?

**Pollux.** Yes, very much; for their religion held that the soul of a body which was unburied was void of any intelligible essence. They believed it was left to the power and command of a raging, furious fantasy <note>original: "phansie"</note> and subject to the torment and affliction of physical qualities. Because the soul was thought to be an airy body, sometimes the departed shadow <note>the ghost or spirit</note> would speak to its remaining friends, and sometimes it would evilly vex and torment its enemies with revenge. As in the poet Virgil, *Dido threatens Aeneas*, saying:

<margin>Aeneid, Book 4.</margin>
->*As a ghost, I will be present in all places; you shall pay the penalty, wicked man.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Omnibus umbra locis adero dabis improbe poenas."</note>
->*My ghost shall everywhere attend you, villain;*<-
->*I will torment you.*<-

<margin>The history of Gaius Caligula.</margin>
Suetonius, as we have shown before, adds a similar account concerning the dead body of the Emperor *Gaius Caligula* in the Garden of *Lamia*. Because his body was not duly buried—being only covered with a light turf—it greatly disquieted and troubled the owners of the garden with violent night-time incursions. This continued until his sisters, who had returned from banishment, took the body up again and buried it with the proper rites.

<margin>The house of Caligula burned because of the spirits.</margin>
**Castor.** And the house where that same Emperor died could be freed from the fury of these shadows or spirits in no other way, as history mentions, except by burning it down.

<margin>The mountain of Hekla <note>original: "Hechelberg"</note>.</margin>
**Pollux.** *Aristotle*, speaking of miracles, mentions a certain mountain in Iceland <note>original: "Norway"; the author likely refers to Mount Hekla</note> named *Hekla*, surrounded by the sea, that continually sent forth such lamentable voices, like the yelling and howling of infernal devils. The noise and clamor of their terrible roaring could be heard almost a mile away, and the flocking together of great ravens and vultures near it prevented anyone from approaching.

He carried some food with him, which he fed upon for a long time. At length, Eric, the King of Sweden <note>original: "Suecia"</note>, came to that place with an army and broke open the tomb of Asuitus, supposing there had been treasure hidden inside. But when the cave was opened, he drew out Asmundus and brought him into the light. Asmundus was covered with a deformed, haggard <note>original: "sharp"</note> countenance, a deathly deformity, and was gored with blood flowing from fresh wounds.

*Castor*. But this story does not pertain to our purpose.

*Pollux*. Truly it does, if you diligently note these verses, which explain the cause of his wounds.

*Castor*. Show me those verses, if you have them.

And all this blood appears still.

His head my sword did from him take,
And his trunk to the ground I fixed with a stake.

### Of the Nature of Spirits.

<margin>The devils have bodies.</margin>
They receive the habit and covering of vegetable bodies <note>original: "vegetable bodies"; likely referring to a lower or more material form of existence compared to pure spirit</note> and are transformed into several kinds of shapes, whereby they can more craftily and subtly delude and deceive the improvident wits of men. *Basil the Great* <note>original: "Basilius Magnus"; a 4th-century Greek bishop and theologian</note> also testifies to the same, and witnesses that they have bodies appropriate to themselves, just as the pure Angels do. *Psellus* <note>Michael Psellos, an 11th-century Byzantine philosopher and politician</note>, a practitioner of the occult <note>original: "Necromancer"</note>, also reports the same; and he also teaches that sometimes they sleep or rest, change their places, and show themselves visible to the senses of men. *Socrates* 
<margin>The Spirits cannot be discerned by sex.</margin>
asserts that a Spirit spoke with him, which he also sometimes saw and felt; but their bodies cannot be discerned to have a difference in sex. However, *Marcus of Chersonesus* <note>original: "Marcus Cherronesus"; a Byzantine writer on demonology</note>, an excellent researcher into the natures of Spirits, writes that they have simple bodies, and that a difference of sex belongs only to compound bodies <note>bodies made of multiple elements, like human flesh</note>. Yet their bodies are easily drawn to motion and flexibility, and are naturally apt to receive every configuration.

<margin>All Spirits cannot receive several shapes.</margin>
For, says he,
> *Just as the clouds show forth the appearance and resemblance sometimes of men, and sometimes of every thing you can conceive; so likewise do the bodies of Spirits receive various shapes as they please. By reason of this, they transform themselves into the forms sometimes of men, and sometimes of women.*

Nevertheless, this ability is not free to them all, but only to the fiery and airy Spirits. For he teaches that the Spirits of the water have slower and less active bodies; because of the slowness and softness of that element, they most especially resemble birds and women, of which kind the *Naiads* and *Nereids* <note>water nymphs from Greek mythology</note> are, as celebrated by the Poets. *Trithemius* <note>original: "Trimetius"; Johannes Trithemius, a German abbot and occultist</note> testifies that the Devils desire to assume the shapes of men rather than any other form, except when they cannot find the matter of the air convenient and befitting for that purpose. And he says that they frame such kinds of appearances for themselves as the available moisture or vapor <note>original: "humour or vapour"</note> will afford; and so they are seen sometimes in the form and shape of a Lion, a Wolf, a Sow, an Ass, a Centaur, or a horned Man having feet like a Goat. It is reported that such beings were seen in the mountain of *Thuringia* <note>original: "Thrungia"; a region in central Germany</note>, where a terrible roaring was heard.

*Castor Porphyrius* in *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian</note>, in his fourth book of *Evangelical Preparation* <note>original: "Evangelical Preparation"; a work of Christian apologetics</note>...

It cites various historical and theological authorities to explain how different types of spirits (airy, fiery, or watery) manifest in different forms, such as animals, mythological creatures, or humans.</summary>

<header>A Discourse 70</header>

*Evangelical Preparations* <note>original: "Evangelical Preparations" refers to the *Praeparatio Evangelica* by Eusebius</note>, teaches that some of these are good spirits and some are bad; but I have considered them to be all evil.

<margin>Pollux.</margin> **Pollux:** Then it seems that you are not seduced by the assertions of either *Porphyry*, *Apuleius*, *Proclus*, or some other Platonists <note>Followers of the philosopher Plato who believed in a hierarchy of spirits</note> who are mentioned in St. *Augustine’s* book *The City of God*, Chapters 2 and 3. They also affirm that some of these spirits are good. However, *Eusebius* in the aforementioned book, Chapter 8, and St. *Augustine* concerning the same in his book *The City of God*, Chapters 8 and 9, use very great and strong arguments to convince the Platonists that none of these demons are good, but all are evil. 

<margin>There is no good demon.</margin> We also confirm this from their names, which are everywhere set forth in Holy Scripture. 

<margin>Why he is called Diabolus.</margin> For the Devil is called *Diabolus*, which means "flowing downwards." This signifies that he who, swelling with pride, determined to reign in high places, fell flowing downwards to the lowest parts like the torrent of a violent stream, as *Cassiodorus* writes. 

<margin>Satan.</margin> And he is called *Satan*, which means "an adversary." As St. *Jerome* testifies, by reason of the corruption of his own malice, he continually resists and is an adversary against God, who is the chief good. 

<margin>Behemoth.</margin> He is called *Behemoth* in the 40th chapter of *Job*, which signifies an ox; for just as an ox desires hay, so he, with the teeth of his suggestions, covets to destroy the upright lives of spiritual men. 

<margin>Leviathan.</margin> And *Leviathan* in the same place, which signifies "an addition," because the Devil always endeavors to add evil to evil and punishment to punishment. 

<margin>Apollyon.</margin> He is also called in *Revelation* 15 *Apollyon*, signifying "a destroyer" <note>original: "rooter out"</note>; for he roots out the virtues which God plants in the soul. 

<margin>A Serpent.</margin> He is called a *Serpent* in the 12th chapter of *Revelation* because of his poisonous nature <note>original: "virulency"</note>. 

<margin>A Lion.</margin> A *Lion* in the 1st Epistle of *Peter*, the last chapter, which roars about seeking whom he may devour. He is called a "cunning workman" in *Isaiah* 54 <note>original: "Isa. 55"</note> because by his malice he tests the vessels that are chosen and approved. 

In *Isaiah* 34, he is called a *Centaur* <note>original: "Onocentaurus"</note>, a *Fury* <note>original: "Erynes"</note>, a *Shaggy Beast* <note>original: "Pilosus"</note>, a *Siren*, a *Vampire* <note>original: "Lamia"</note>, a *Screech Owl* <note>original: "Ulula"</note>, and an *Ostrich* <note>original: "Struthio"</note>. And by *David* in the 91st Psalm <note>original: "99 Psal."</note>, he is called an *Asp*, a *Basilisk*, and a *Dragon*. In the Gospel, he is called *Mammon*, the Prince of this world, and the Ruler of darkness.

**Castor.**

# Of the Nature of Spirits.
<page-num>129</page-num>

**Castor.** Why, therefore, have the theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> declared that the Almighty has given two kinds of spirits to men—the one good, the keeper and preserver of their lives, and the other evil, resisting the good—if they are all evil?

**Pollux.** The holy Doctors <note>referring to the Doctors of the Church, or authoritative Christian teachers</note> understand the "good spirit" to be a good angel, such as we read *Raphael* was to *Tobias*. Raphael bound the evil spirit *Asmodeus* in the wilderness of the furthest parts of *Egypt* so that Tobias might be safer.

**Castor.** It would have been safer for every man to have been without the evil spirits entirely; what, therefore, was the will of the heavenly Father concerning them?

**Pollux.** His will was that, by the assistance of the good spirits, we might courageously wage continual war against the evil spirits. Being clothed with the armor <note>original: "harnesse"</note> of righteousness like valiant soldiers, we may gird our loins with truth and, with the shield of faith, resist and fight against all the devil's darts.

**Castor.** If we agree to this warfare of spirits, it seems right to inquire whether the devils have the power of doing harm granted to them by God, or whether they can hurt as much as they please on their own?

<margin>The devils are the princes of the earth.</margin> **Pollux.** If the latter were true, who could imagine an end to their harming? But it is very manifest that their authority from on high is of such great significance that *John the Evangelist* does not hesitate to name the devils the "Princes of the earth." <note>original: "the earth," likely referring to the "Prince of this world" mentioned in the Gospel of John.</note>

**Castor.** In what manner, therefore, do they cause harm?

**Pollux.** Although they are most mighty and powerful spirits, yet they can do no harm unless it is by permission; or, as John of Damascus <note>original: "Damascenus"</note> says, "By dispensation." And *Chrysostom* says, "*They have a limited power; for truly, without the will of God, they cannot touch a hair of any man's head.*" The Devil could not have deceived the prophets of *Ahab* if he had not received power from God; neither could he have brought any harm <note>original: "detriment"</note> upon *Job*, either to his body or his goods, except by the power God had given him. In the 7th chapter of *Exodus*, the magicians made frogs and serpents by the permissive power of the Devil; but lice they could not bring forth, by reason of the greater power...

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the discussion of God's power over demonic magic.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the dual nature of spirits (good and evil) and establishes that demons have no independent power to cause harm without God's permission. It cites biblical examples like Job, Ahab, and the magicians of Egypt to illustrate the limits of demonic influence.</summary>

the power of God prohibiting them. Neither in the Gospel could the devils hurt the swine until Christ had given them leave.

**Castor.** Therefore, the devil is not so much to be feared as the Lord our God. We should pray either that He would not suffer the devil to rage against us, or if at any time by His own determined counsel He lets loose the devil's chains, that He would then defend and mercifully preserve us.

**Pollux.** You say well; for just as a wild boar is not to be feared if he is bound and held with a strong chain by a powerful man who is able to restrain the boar's fierceness, but rather the man is to be feared and petitioned not to let the boar loose; so also Satan is not to be feared, being bound with the cords of the Almighty. Rather, we should fear the Almighty, who holds him with a cord, lest at any time He should slacken that cord to execute His will against us.

<margin>The devil's corruption where the Word is not yet known.</margin> **Castor.** We know that the devils, after the incarnation of the Word <note>The "Word" refers to Jesus Christ, based on John 1:1.</note>, were called the "lords of the earth"; but I wonder, where the Word is not yet incarnate <note>Meaning where the Gospel has not yet been preached or accepted.</note>, whether they have power over men there as well.

**Pollux.** If it pleases God, they have very much power. But take a demonstration of this, Castor, from the Chaldeans; among whom the devil raged with so much power and dominion that they had no respect for the true God, but instead worshipped the elements. There is no need for a demonstration regarding the Greeks, for the fury of the devil reigned so much among them that, by his arguments, they considered Saturn a great god—even though he devoured his own children. They named Jupiter, an adulterer and father of all filthiness, to be the "father of gods and men." Bacchus, the most wicked example of all servitude and bondage, they called a "free father." Venus, a prostitute, they termed a pure virgin; and they worshipped Flora, a harlot, as a type or example of virginity. There is no man ignorant of the fact that the Egyptians have been worse than the Greeks, since they made peculiar gods for themselves through the lifeless persuasions of the devil: for one...

### Of the Nature of Spirits.

One person worshipped a sheep, another a goat, and another a calf. Very many worshipped hogs, crows, hawks, vultures, eagles, crocodiles, cats, dogs, wolves, asses, and dragons. They even worshipped growing things, such as onions, garlic, and thorns. Anyone who is eager for reading shall find these accounts in *Damascenus* <note>John of Damascus, an 8th-century Christian monk and scholar</note>, in his *History of Josaphat and Barlaam*, and in *Eusebius*, in the fourth book and first chapter of *Evangelical Preparations* <note>original: "Praeparatio Evangelica," a work by the Bishop Eusebius of Caesarea intended to prove the superiority of Christianity over paganism</note>. 

Neither do I consider the *Hebrews* (who glory in being the offspring of their father *Abraham*) to have been any better than the former groups. By the instinct of the devil, after their coming up out of *Egypt*, they violently assaulted the Prophets and holy men of God with cruel hands, whom they eventually killed. I will remain silent on how diligently they also introduced the gods—or rather, the devils—of the *Gentiles* <note>Non-Jewish nations or pagans</note> into their own religion.

*Castor*. I perceive by these assertions of yours that various devils have been adored as gods. For you have now said that the *Greeks*, through the madness with which the devil possessed them, created for themselves *Saturn*, *Jupiter*, *Bacchus*, *Venus*, and *Flora* as gods. *Lactantius* <note>An early Christian author and advisor to Constantine the Great</note>, in his fourth book *On True Wisdom* <note>original: "De vera Sapientia"</note>, also considers these to be devils.

*Pollux*. Tell me, I pray, the words of *Lactantius*.

*Castor*. Mark them; they are as follows: "The same devils are the gods of the *Gentiles*; but if anyone will not believe these things from me, then let him believe *Homer*, who joins the great *Jupiter* to the great devils. Other poets and philosophers also call them sometimes gods and sometimes devils, of which one is a true name and the other false. For the most wicked spirits, when they are conjured <note>term: "conjured" - summoned or compelled by an oath or ritual</note>, confess themselves to be devils; but where they are worshipped, they declare themselves to be gods. They do this so that they may thrust men into errors and draw them from the worship of the true God, through whom alone eternal death can be escaped."

*Pollux*. It is necessary for me now to be more inquisitive in this discourse: is power given to the devils to foretell things to come? Concerning this matter, I have not yet been able to hit the right mark <note>original: "dart at the right mark," meaning to reach a correct understanding or conclusion</note>.

For this question seems sufficiently doubtful to me.

They do this because, in the perception of their airy bodies, they have a far more strong and prevalent power of foreknowledge than men with earthly bodies can have. Or, it is because of the incomparable swiftness of their airy bodies, which wonderfully exceeds not only the speed of men and wild beasts, but also the flight of birds. By this means, they are able to announce things long before they become known to us; we, by reason of the earthly slowness of our senses, never cease to marvel at and admire this. Or, it is because of the benefit of their continual life that they obtain this wonderful experience of things, which we cannot attain because of the shortness of our momentary life, which is, as it were, but a bubble.

**Pollux:** This last assertion of Saint *Augustine* seems more true to me than the rest, because the passing of many years brings great experience.

**Pollux:** Why, therefore, do the devils so willingly and of their own accord undertake prophecies and answer oracles? What benefit do they get from this?

Nevertheless, even if he <note>The devil.</note> understands that he has not been granted the grace within himself to foretell the truth, he foretells things anyway; but so obscurely, says Saint Augustine, that he always lays the blame for the things he predicted upon the person interpreting them. Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305), a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote "Philosophy from Oracles."</note>, in his book of *Oracles*, although he is the greatest supporter of devils and the most expert teacher of diabolical arts, nevertheless says along with the aforementioned Doctors <note>term: Doctors; here meaning "Doctors of the Church" or learned authorities.</note> that the foreknowledge of things to come is not only complex for men, but also uncertain to the gods, and full of many obscurities.

Castor: What is a miracle, Pollux?

<margin>What a miracle is.</margin>
Pollux: A new and unusual event which happens contrary to its normal course and custom, and draws men into admiration of it.

Castor: But do they work miracles?

<margin>The devils work miracles.</margin>
Pollux: They do. For by what other means do you believe that Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of medicine.</note> was honored as a god in his consecration, if not only by means of a miracle? This happened when her wooden image was asked by Furius Camillus <note>A Roman soldier and statesman (c. 446–365 BC) who, according to legend, relocated the statue of Juno from Veii to Rome.</note> whether it would be carried...

<margin>Why God permits the devils to work miracles.</margin>
**Pollux:** *Firmianus* <note>Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author often called the "Christian Cicero"</note> excellently shows why God does so in his penultimate book on the works of God, *On the Workmanship of God* <note>original: *De opificio Dei*</note>. For he says that virtue is not virtue unless it has some opposition, in ruling over which it may show and exercise its power. For he says, "As victory cannot exist without struggle <note>original: "Vertue"</note>, so neither can virtue subsist without an enemy." As soon as the Almighty endowed man with virtue, He immediately added an enemy to him, lest that virtue should lose its nature by being numbed with idleness. He says that a man cannot otherwise attain to the highest step unless he always has an active hand, and that he shall establish and build up his salvation through continual warfare and contention. For God does not will that mortal men shall reach immortal blessedness by an easy journey; rather, he must wrestle and strive with sails and oars against the author and inventor of all evils and errors, who causes and works execrable things and miracles.

<margin>Sometimes it happens that the devil cannot be resisted.</margin>
**Castricius:** But sometimes it happens that, by reason of the subtle snares and stratagems of the devil—which he so craftily prepares against us, and especially against simple persons whom he entangles with vain religions—we cannot resist him. Or, if we suppose ourselves to be very able to withstand him, we may nevertheless be greatly deceived by him; as we read he often did to the good, but almost foolish, shepherd <note>original: "Pastor"</note> mentioned by *Trithemius* <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot, lexicographer, and chronicler known for his writings on magic and cryptography</note>.

The fire cannot be quenched. Even when it is secured by this means, it may not be gathered or plucked up without danger; for immediate death follows the person who gathers or plucks it up, unless he is protected by a preservative <note>original: "Preservative"; likely an amulet or medicinal charm.</note> worn about his neck made of that same root. For this reason, those who desire the root dig a trench around it; then, having tied a cord around the root, they tie the other end to a dog and suddenly walk away. Consequently, the dog, struggling to follow its master, pulls up the root; and, as if the dog were performing the task in place of its master, it dies immediately. Afterward, the root may be taken and handled without any danger to anyone. The same *Josephus* teaches that this root has such immediate power for expiations <note>original: "expiations"; here meaning the driving out of evil or ritual purification.</note> that all those who are vexed and tormented by unclean spirits are immediately delivered if they carry this root with them.

He also writes concerning the fire which is extinguished with oil and kindled with cold water when it is sprinkled over it.

He persuades us to meditate upon all those things which the devil devises and invents to overthrow our integrity, so that his failure may prove the glory of our constancy. We must be willing to constantly war against all these schemes <note>original: "machinations"</note>, which are permitted by God for this purpose. And the Prophet *Jeremiah* teaches the same in these words: *The Lord of hosts is the one who approves the just.*

*D. Maximus*, in his book *On Charity* <note>original: "de charitate"</note>, commands us to bind and kill the devils. He says we bind them when, by diligent observation of the Commandments of God, we diminish and suppress those passions <note>original: "affections"</note> that boil up within us. And we are said to kill them when we so truly deaden <note>original: "mortifie"</note> our lusts that we cut the devil off from all opportunities to accuse us, saying with the Prophet: *Depart, O murderer; the Lord, the strong warrior, is with me. <note>The author refers here to a commentator on Eusebius or a similar historian.</note> He who interprets the *Ecclesiastical History* <note>original: "Ecclesiastical History," likely referring to the history of the early Christian Church</note> says that all sensual appetites are to be shut out and excluded, so that the devil may not be admitted—neither by the allurements of the eyes, nor by itching ears, nor by the petulance and stubbornness of a hurtful tongue. Some admonish us, in our going to war against the devil, to use two sorts of weapons: the one is pure prayer, which may raise up our affections unto heaven; and the other is true and perfect knowledge, which may communicate and fill our understandings with wholesome doctrines. This knowledge may suggest to us what we are to pray for, so that we may pray ardently, according to St. James, and not doubtingly. In the Prophecy of Isaiah and the Epistles of St. Paul, we may find the same things (Isaiah 59, Ephesians 6, and 1 Thessalonians 5), which may serve as a remedy against vain ghosts, so that they may be expelled.

**Poll.** For a remedy against ghosts? Do you conceive that a ghost is distinct and different from a spirit?

**Cast.** I truly do not know what I should think of this. For, floating in so spacious a sea of many opinions, I am so led by doubt that I cannot easily reach a certain harbor of judgment. There are some who suppose that these ghosts are devils, because of the great fear and terror with which they violently harass men by night in their houses, and sometimes do harm because of their innate nature. There are others who believe these spirits are deceitful fantasies, deceiving those who hold wrong beliefs; they believe these spirits deceive and frighten the inhabitants in their houses through fallacious visions and imaginations. These people deny that they are spirits indeed, because spirits have a body without hands and feet; therefore, they argue, they can hurt no man nor make any noise. These people are ignorant that the Angel (who also has a body without hands and feet) carried Habakkuk <note>A reference to the story of Bel and the Dragon in the Apocrypha, where an angel transports the prophet.</note> with his whole dinner, by the hair of his head, into Babylon, and afterwards brought him back again and set him in his own place. Neither do they consider that the Spirit of the Lord, also without a physical body, snatched up Philip and carried him to Azotus <note>Acts 8:39-40</note>. I shall forbear to speak—

<summary>This page discusses spiritual defenses against the devil through prayer and knowledge, while debating whether "ghosts" are actual demonic entities or merely psychological fantasies.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual warfare, demonology, ghosts vs. spirits, Habakkuk, Philip the Evangelist, discernment of spirits</keywords>

# Of the Nature of Spirits.

I will speak concerning a certain incorporeal spirit which so disquieted the house of my grandfather that, for a period of almost thirty years, he caused it to be uninhabitable—unless a lamp was kept burning inside. Even then, that did not sufficiently quiet the spirit; for when people left the house, they were so molested with stones thrown from above in the streets that the spirits would even knock the hearts of pine trees <note>The resinous center of the pine, used as a primitive torch.</note>, which they used for torches, out of their hands. Concerning the ghost that haunted the house of *Athenodorus* <note>original: Anthenodorus; refers to the Stoic philosopher Athenodorus Cananites, whose encounter with a ghost in Athens was famously recorded by Pliny the Younger.</note> the philosopher, and the tumultuous spirit of *Gaius Caligula* <note>The Roman Emperor; Suetonius recorded that his ghost haunted the Lamian Gardens until his body was properly buried.</note>, more could be said; but you have already understood the accounts of them in the previous discourse. From all of this, we may easily refute the opinions of those who deny that spirits can walk or make any motion. However, listen now to how much truth we may find in the assertions of those who suppose that these tumultuous spirits are neither devils nor phantasms, but the souls of the dead.

**Pollux:** Are there people who hold that opinion?

**Castor:** There are those who hold both opinions. For they declare that these are the souls of those who have departed from their bodies laden and clogged with their sins. They are therefore heard to be more or less turbulent in houses, according to whether they have a more or less sensible, burning spark of that sin remaining. Thus, unless they are meanwhile expelled and driven away from there, or purified by alms or intercessions <note>Prayers made on behalf of another.</note>, they are confined to a certain limit of liberty, wandering in those areas in expectation of the Last Judgment.

**Pollux:** Why is that?

<margin>Lactantius on the souls of the dead.</margin>**Castor:** Because I believe that the souls of those who sleep in Christ live with Christ and do not wander about the earth; and the souls of those who are oppressed and burdened with the heavy weight of their sins are bound with Satan in the chains of darkness, awaiting judgment in hell.

**Pollux:** But *Firmianus* <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine I.</note>, a writer of significant judgment, thinks the contrary in the book he wrote *Concerning the Divine Reward* <note>original: "de Divino premio"; likely referring to "Divinae Institutiones" (Divine Institutes), specifically the sections on the soul's reward.</note>.

<summary>The speakers debate whether haunting spirits are demons or the souls of the deceased, referencing historical hauntings and the theological state of the soul after death.</summary>
<keywords>ghosts, Athenodorus, Caligula, purgatory, Lactantius, afterlife, demonology, haunting</keywords>

**Castor:** How is that?

**Pollux:** These are his words: "Let no man believe that
<margin>The opinion of Firmianus <note>Lactantius Firmianus (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author.</note>.</margin> the souls of the dead are judged immediately after death. For they are all detained in one common custody until the time comes when the Almighty Judge shall examine and investigate their deeds. Then, those who are found righteous shall receive the reward of immortality; but those whose sins and wickedness are then detected shall not rise again, but shall be enclosed with the wicked in darkness and destined for eternal punishments."

**Castor:** St. Augustine agrees with Lactantius in his *Enchiridion* <note>original: *Enchiridion*, a handbook on Christian faith, hope, and love</note>, saying that the time which passes between the death of mankind and the final resurrection holds the souls in secret, hidden receptacles. There, every soul receives appropriate <note>original: "condigne"</note> rest or misery for the good or evil they did in the body while they lived.

**Pollux:** Nor does St. Ambrose disagree with this. In his second book of *Cain and Abel*, he says that the soul is loosened from the body and, after the end of this life, is suspended until the uncertain <note>original: "ambiguous"</note> time of the final judgment.

**Castor:** So also some have declared that the soul of Emperor Trajan <note>original: "Trajanus Cæsar"</note> wandered about; but the soul of St. George was freed from such a delay <note>original: "suffrage," here meaning a state of waiting or intercessory period</note>.

**Pollux:** You have just now said, and truly so, that the sea of various opinions concerning these spirits is vast. Indeed it is. But I desire you to tell me—and I hope it is not a trouble to do so—which "port" you are heading for; for I am not yet satisfied of the certainty of this matter by our conversation.

**Castor:** What you desire to know, I believe, is this: I hold that these restless <note>original: "tumultuous"</note> spirits are merely images of Satan. They are not to be feared, nor is any credit to be given to their answers. They are in no way the souls of the dead, who either live with Christ if they have done well, or else are bound in chains with Satan if they have done evil.

**Pollux:** It remains for us to examine <note>original: "sift out"</note> this, Castor; for it happens...

<summary>Castor and Pollux cite Church Fathers like Lactantius, Augustine, and Ambrose to argue that souls wait in a hidden state until the final judgment, concluding that "ghostly" apparitions are demonic deceptions rather than actual souls of the deceased.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue between Castor and Pollux regarding the nature of spirits and whether the appearance of the dead in dreams constitutes a real spiritual visitation.</meta>

# Of the Nature of Spirits

It happens now sometimes that my father appears to me in my sleep; perhaps that may also seem to you to be a spirit.

**Castor:** It may seem so, but I will not contradict you in anything beyond reason. I will add nothing of my own invention, but at the very least, I will challenge your opinion with the assertions of St. Augustine.

**Pollux:** What assertions are those?

**Castor:** In his eleventh book, which he titles *On Care for the Dead* <note>original: *De mortuorum cura*</note>, he offers these arguments, saying: "Human weakness <note>original: *Humane infirmity*</note> is so prone to believe this of itself, that when someone sees a person who is dead in their sleep, they suppose they are seeing the soul of that dead person. But when they dream of someone who is alive, they are then certain that it was neither the person's soul nor their body, but only the likeness of the man that appeared to them. It is as if they are ignorant that the souls of dead men do not appear to them in dreams, but only the likenesses of the deceased persons." 

He proves both of these points with two examples that occurred at Milan <note>original: *Mediolanus*</note>. In the first, he shows there was the image of a certain father who was dead, who appeared to his son, warning him that he should not pay a debt a second time to an unfair creditor—a debt which the father had already paid before he died. He says the case was this: the father had paid a debt to a certain creditor; after the father's death, the creditor attempted to use force to recover the same money again from the son, who did not know the payment had been made. The image of his father appeared to the son while he was sleeping and showed him where the receipt <note>original: "the Writing"</note> was hidden. Upon waking from his sleep, the son searched for the paper in the place he was directed and found it, thereby defeating the malice of his deceitful creditor. 

The second example is one by which the same St. Augustine shows that the living appear to the living in their sleep. He says that Eulogius the Rhetorician, while teaching the rhetoric of Cicero at Carthage, found a difficult and obscure passage that had not been explained to him. Because of this, he troubled himself both while awake and asleep due to his ignorance. But, on a certain night, the image of Aurelius Augustine appeared to him and taught him the meaning.

<summary>Castor cites St. Augustine to argue that dreams of the dead are merely mental likenesses rather than actual spirits, providing historical examples of such "images" appearing to solve problems for the living.</summary>
<keywords>St. Augustine, dreams, spirits, ghosts, Milan, Carthage, debt, rhetoric, Cicero, visions</keywords>

him in what manner the dark and difficult passage was to be understood.

Poll. *Augustine* does therefore conclude, without doubt, that they are not souls.

Cast. He does so conclude; and the more to strengthen such a judgment, he adds that if the souls of the dead had any interest or role in the affairs of the living, he knew beyond doubt that his own pious mother would not have deserted him. For even one night, when she was living, she followed him both by sea and land; nor did he at any time suffer any anguish of heart but that she comforted his sorrows. And so that this may not seem too harsh a statement, the minister <note>original: "president of Christ"</note> teaches that those people do not err who affirm that the good Angels—by the appointment of God and Divine providence <note>original: "dispensation"</note>—do sometimes come to and visit men, both while they are living and sleeping. Sometimes they even go to the place where souls endure punishment. Notwithstanding, this is not granted to all, but only to those who have lived in such a way that God judges them worthy of this mercy; or to those upon whom, without any respect to their own merits, God is pleased to glorify his unspeakable mercy, so that by the prayers of the living they may obtain pardon for their sins and deliverance from the prison of torments <note>This refers to the concept of Purgatory and the efficacy of intercessory prayer</note>.

Cast. That is certainly true; nor does he show himself to be offended by those who leave all these things to the unsearchable judgments of God and do not labor to find out the secrets thereof.

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>Of the Nature of Spirits.</header>

## Of the Nature of Spirits.

Consider the writings of St. *Augustine*. For that great prelate of the Christian Church writes, in the 11th chapter of his book *On the Trinity* <note>original: "de Trinitate"</note>, that it is a very easy thing for wicked spirits—because of the airy substance of their bodies—to perform many things that seem wonderful to souls oppressed by earthly bodies. He also says that earthly bodies may be so trained through art and exercise that they can perform such wonderful things in public theaters that those who have never seen them would not believe they were possible. Such people might assume these feats were done by the assistance of the devil and his ministers to make their bodies of such an airy element that the flesh wonders at it. Or else—which is a significant point—he says also that they contrive to delude human senses with hidden inspirations, forms, and fantasies of images, by which people may be deceived whether waking or sleeping. Thus far Augustine. But, if you wish, I will also produce another witness beyond reproach: *Pollux*.

Pollux: I would like you to tell me who that is.

Castor: *Abbot Trithemius* <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot, lexicographer, and chronicler often associated with the occult.</note>, in his third question to *St. Maximus Emilianus* (which was mentioned before), says this: "The devils, among unfaithful people, seem to raise the dead to life and show miracles to curious men, so that they might—as it were—swallow them up with error instead of miracles. They are altogether stubborn and obstinate; <margin>The devils cannot really raise the dead.</margin> but they cannot truly and really raise the dead. Instead, they variously deceive the senses of men by showing them faked resemblances of the dead. For it is certainly manifest that the devils can do all things, but only in a false imitation of true, holy miracles."

Pollux: Some say that the devils are obedient to wicked men because of the similarity of their malice. How does that seem to you?

Castor: It seems to me that they are obedient to evil men, but not to all men.

Pollux: But to whom?

Castor: Certainly to those with whom they have contracted and made compacts and covenants; such as those women who are called <term>Pythonists</term> <note>A term for a person possessed by a spirit of divination or a witch, derived from the Pythia of the Delphic Oracle.</note>, who have vowed themselves by promise unto him.

<summary>Castor cites St. Augustine and Abbot Trithemius to argue that demons use their "airy" nature to create illusions and fake miracles, such as the appearance of raising the dead, to deceive humans. He further explains that spirits only obey those who have entered into specific contracts or covenants with them.</summary>

<keywords>St. Augustine, Abbot Trithemius, demonology, illusions, necromancy, Pythonists, spiritual contracts, miracles</keywords>

**Pollux.** But although they are compelled to be so serviceable unto them, yet is this service true or feigned?

**Castor.** It is feigned, certainly; for they are subservient unto men of their own accord and genuine work only so that they may deceive them and allure them to themselves. Although we do not deny that their service is sometimes true, but only towards those men whose faith in the Lord Jesus Christ—by the merit of His holiness—has caused them to be acceptable and friends unto Him. And *Lactantius* <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine I.</note> also testifies to this in these words, in his second book *On the Origin of Error* <note>original: "De origine Erroris"</note>, Chapter 16: "That the devils do fear the just—that is, those that worship God—in whose Name they are conjured to depart out of bodies, and by whose words they are beaten as if with scourges. And they do not only confess that they are devils, but they declare their names; neither can they lie unto the just." And the same *Lactantius*, in his fourth book *On True Wisdom* <note>original: "De vera Sapientia"</note>, Chapter 27, says: "That it is necessary that they who are of the true Christian Religion should know the course and order of the devils, and understand their subtlety, and restrain their force, and conquer and subdue them with spiritual weapons, and force them to obey them."

**Pollux.** I am now by you sufficiently informed of all things which I have hitherto desired to know; therefore, I shall not any further trouble you with my questions, or rather riddles, but leave you to your own business.

**Castor.** Neither have I considered myself idle in answering you; but let the use of this conversation yield us each to the other an equal recompense. Farewell, therefore.

**Pollux.** And you also.

->GERARD<-

## GERARD OF CREMONA
### ON ASTRONOMICAL GEOMANCY.

<insert>B</insert>ecause Astronomy is such a transcendent and subtle art in itself, a person must consider so many factors before they can attain true judgment through it. Because the eye of the understanding cannot pierce even halfway into its depths, and few doctors of our recent times have been found experienced enough to know sufficiently how to judge by it, I have composed this work. I wish for it to be named *Astronomical Geomancy*, wherein I will sufficiently teach how to judge with less labor and study. For in this present science, it is not necessary to look up the Ascendant <note>The sign of the Zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment.</note> or the hour in a table, as is required in Astrology.

It is necessary, therefore, to make four unequal lines by randomly setting down points, and then to join those points together. From the points that are not joined together—those which remain at the ends of the lines (as is done in standard Geomancy)—extract one figure. Then, take the sign of the Zodiac that corresponds to that figure and set it as the Ascendant for the sake of the reading. 

If *Acquisitio* <note>Latin: "Gain" or "Acquisition." A geomantic figure associated with profit.</note> arises from the ends of those four lines, let *Aries* be placed in the Ascendant. If *Laetitia* <note>Latin: "Joy."</note> or the Lesser Fortune <note>original: *Fortuna Minor*</note> arises, put *Taurus* in the Ascendant. If *Puer* <note>Latin: "Boy."</note> or *Rubeus* <note>Latin: "Red."</note> arises, place *Gemini*. If *Albus* <note>Latin: "White."</note> arises, place *Cancer*. If *Via* <note>Latin: "Way" or "Path."</note> arises, place *Leo*. If *Conjunctio* <note>Latin: "Conjunction."</note> or the *Dragon’s Head* <note>The North Node of the Moon, known in Latin as *Caput Draconis*.</note> arises, place *Virgo*. If *Puella* <note>Latin: "Girl."</note> arises, place *Libra*. If *Amissio* <note>Latin: "Loss."</note> or *Tristitia* <note>Latin: "Sorrow."</note>...

<summary>Gerard of Cremona introduces "Astronomical Geomancy," a simplified divinatory system that uses geomantic figures to determine an astrological Ascendant without the need for complex astronomical tables. He provides a key for mapping specific geomantic figures, such as Acquisitio and Laetitia, to the signs of the Zodiac.</summary>

# Gerard of Cremona
<note>Gerard of Cremona (c. 1114–1187) was a famous translator of scientific and Arabic works into Latin; this text attributes a system of "Geomantic Astrology" to him.</note>

If the figure is *Laetitia* <note>original: "Laetitia"; a geomantic figure representing joy</note>, the Ascendant is Scorpio; if it is the Dragon's Tail <note>original: "Cauda Draconis"</note>, the Ascendant is Sagittarius; if *Populus* <note>the People</note>, the Ascendant is Capricorn; if *Fortuna Major* <note>Greater Fortune</note>, the Ascendant is Aquarius; if *Carcer* <note>original: "Cancer", likely a typo for the geomantic figure Carcer, meaning the Prison</note>, then set Pisces as the Ascendant. 

Afterward, in the second House, place the sign which immediately follows the first. In the third House, place the third Sign, and continue placing the rest in order until you reach the end of the Signs. Create one square figure divided into twelve equal parts, and place the Signs in order within it, just as is done in Astrology and as you can see in this diagram. In this method, we do not need to consider the *witnesses* or the *Judge* <note>term: Witnesses and Judge; these are specific roles for figures in a standard geomantic reading which are ignored in this specific astrological conversion</note>, or any other rules belonging to Geomancy. We only use the sixteen Figures so that we may determine the twelve Signs to which they correspond. Observe the layout of the Figure as it is placed here.

<page-num>149</page-num>

## Of Astronomical Geomancy.

Afterward, it is necessary to make four lines in sequence for every planet by randomly marking down points. You must do the same for the *Dragon’s Head* <note>The North Node of the Moon</note>, just as you did for the Ascendant. Divide those points by twelve; the remainder—or twelve itself if there is no remainder—is the number you keep. Place the planet for which the calculation was made into the House corresponding to that remaining number. That is, if twelve remains, let the planet be placed in the twelfth House; if ten, in the tenth House; if one, in the first House; if two, in the second House, and so on for the rest. 

You should always begin with the *Sun*, followed by the *Moon*, then *Venus* and *Mercury*, then *Saturn*, *Jupiter*, and *Mars*, and finally the *Dragon’s Head* and *Dragon’s Tail* <note>The North and South Nodes of the Moon</note>. However, you must always take care that you do not ask a question during rainy, cloudy, or very windy weather, nor when you are angry or your mind is preoccupied with many affairs. Do not perform this for those who wish to test or mock you, nor should you repeat the same question under the same figure or form, for that leads to error.

### Questions of the First House.

If you desire to know concerning the life of any man, whether it shall be long or short, look to the Lord of the Ascendant <note>The planet that rules the sign on the cusp of the first house</note>. If he is in strong "Angles" <note>The 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses, considered the most powerful positions</note>, it signifies a long life. If he is in "Succedents" <note>The 2nd, 5th, 8th, and 11th houses, which follow the angles</note>, it signifies a middle age. If he is in "Cadent" houses <note>The 3rd, 6th, 9th, and 12th houses, considered the weakest positions</note>, it signifies a short life. 

Furthermore, if he is in strong Angles, he signifies "greater years"; if in Succedents, "mean" <note>average</note> years; and if in Cadents, "lesser" years. 
*   The lesser years of *Saturn* are thirty, the mean are forty-four, and the greater are fifty-eight. 
*   The lesser years of *Jupiter* are twelve, the mean are forty, and the greater are forty-seven. 
*   The lesser years of *Mars* are fifteen, his mean years are forty, and the greater years are forty-seven. 
*   The lesser years of the *Sun* are nineteen, his mean years are forty-five, and his greater years are eighty-two. 
*   The lesser years of *Venus* are eight, her mean years are forty-five, and her greater years are eighty-two. 
*   *Mercury's* lesser years are twenty, his...

means years are forty-nine, and his greater years are eighty. The lesser years of the **Moon** are fifteen, her mean years are thirty-nine, and her greater years are a hundred and seven. <note>In traditional astrology, each planet was assigned "years" (lesser, mean, and greater) used to predict the length of a person's life or the duration of an event.</note> And also look to see if **Mars** or **Saturn** are in the first House, and the Lord of the eighth House is with them; and if the **Sun** is in the eighth, the Querent <term>the person asking the astrological question</term> shall not live. Likewise, if the Lord of the Ascendant <term>the planet ruling the rising sign</term> happens to be "void of course" <note>original: "void of courſe"; an astrological term meaning a planet does not make any further major aspects before leaving its current sign</note>, and **Mars** is in the eighth, the Querent shall not live; but if the **Sun** and the **Moon** are in conjunction in the seventh House, and **Venus** is in the second, he shall live well.

The accidents <term>the specific characteristics or events</term> of the nativity <term>birth chart</term> are likewise to be considered. If you find **Saturn** or **Mercury** in the first House, *he is foolish and talkative*; if it is **Mars** and **Mercury**, he will not be servile, but a wrangler and scoffer; if the **Sun** and **Mercury**, he will be a speaker of truth; and if the **Sun** is in **Aries**, he will apply himself to learn whatever he hears; if **Venus** is in the seventh, he will be luxurious; and if **Saturn**, **Mercury**, and **Venus** are in their fall <term>positions where their influence is weakened</term>, he will be a Sodomite <note>In this historical context, a term used for someone engaging in non-procreative or "unnatural" sexual acts</note>; if the **Sun** and **Venus** are in the tenth, and the **Moon** in the first, he will be very liberal <term>generous</term>; if **Venus**, **Mercury**, and the **Dragon's Head** <term>the North Node of the Moon</term> are in the first, he will be covetous; if the **Moon** and **Mars** are in the first, he will be subject to great bondage; and if **Mars** is Lord of the nativity, he will be rich, and an evil speaker, and litigious; and if the **Sun** is in the first, he will be envious, having a fair body, not very lean, nor very fat; and if **Venus** is in the first, he will be white and fair; if **Mercury** is in the first, he will not be stable, but always in motion; but if the **Moon** be found there, it denotes him to have a graceful face, breast, and arms; if **Saturn** be there, the man will be black and filthy <note>original: "black and filthy"; likely referring to a dark, sallow complexion and a neglected appearance</note>; if **Jupiter**, he will have a round face, a fair forehead, and a ruddy complexion mixed with a little white. 

If you would know his office or art: if the **Moon** is in the seventh with **Saturn**, or in the fourth, or in the tenth, or in the first, it is not good for him to build any house in a city, nor to build a ship, neither is it good for him to be a tiller of land, or to dress vines, or plant trees; but he should be employed in some office belonging to the water, or concerning marriages, or—

If *Mars*, he inquires out of some fear, or about an enemy, death, sickness, riches, or substance.

## Questions of the Second House <note>The Second House in astrology governs personal wealth, possessions, and debts.</note>

1. If you wish to be informed concerning the wealth <note>original: "substance"</note> of any man—whether he shall be rich or not—observe the Lord of the Second House. If that Lord is positioned with a beneficial planet, and there is also a beneficial planet within the Second House itself, he shall be rich. But if the Lord of the Second is joined with malevolent <note>original: "evil"</note> planets, and a malevolent planet is located in the Second House, he shall be poor.

If you would know whether you shall recover a lent item or not, look to see if there is a malevolent planet in the Second House that disagrees with its Lord; if so, then he who withholds the lent item will not willingly return it. But if there is a beneficial planet in the Second House that agrees with its Lord, the item shall be easily recovered. 

If the Lord of the Second House is exalted <note>term: Exalted; a state where a planet is at its maximum strength and influence.</note> and is malevolent, or if a malevolent planet is with him in the Second, or if the Lord of the Second is exalted, then he who keeps the deposited item will not willingly restore it; however, he shall be forced to do so whether he wishes to or not. If a malevolent planet is in the Second House, the item is to be recovered. But if Mercury is in the Second House—provided he is its Lord and brings opposition—then it shall be recovered; and if a beneficial planet is in the Second House, he signifies recovery, even if he is the Lord of that house.

Mark, therefore, the harmony and discord of the planets: the Moon and Jupiter are friends; the Moon and Mars are enemies. Mercury and the Sun are friends; Mercury and Venus are enemies. Venus and Jupiter are friends; Jupiter and the Moon are enemies.

The planets are said to be friends when they agree in the same nature and quality—such as Mars and the Sun, because both of their natures are hot and dry. Venus and the Moon agree in cold and moisture. Planets also agree when they are similar in substance and nature, as Jupiter and Venus are friends; or when the House of one is the exaltation of another, or vice versa.

## Questions of the Third House <note>The Third House governs siblings, short journeys, and communication.</note>

1. If you desire to know how many brothers a man has, look to the Lord of the Third House. It is held that the Querent <note>term: Querent; the person asking the question of the astrologer.</note> has as many brothers as the number of planets to which that Lord is joined.

### Of Astronomical Geomancy.

<note>original: "Jupiter hath"</note> Jupiter has; and the masculine planets signify brothers, and the female planets sisters. Note that *Saturn*, *Mars*, the *Sun*, *Jupiter*, and the *Dragon’s Head* <note>The North Node of the Moon</note> are masculine; but the *Moon*, *Venus*, and the *Dragon’s Tail* <note>The South Node of the Moon</note> are feminine. *Mercury* is promiscuous <note>meaning adaptable or neutral</note>, being sometimes masculine and sometimes feminine: he is masculine when he is joined to masculine planets, or when he is in a masculine quarter of the *Zodiac*; and he is feminine when he is joined to feminine planets, or when he is in a feminine quarter of the *Zodiac*.

->Questions of the Fourth House.<-

If you would know whether it is good for you to stay in any land, city, village, territory, or house, or not, look to the Lord of the *Ascendant* <note>The planet ruling the first house</note>, the fourth house, and the seventh house. If the Lord of the fourth is in the seventh and is well-placed, and the Lords of the first and the tenth house are well-placed and with good planets, then it is good for you to continue in that place where you are. But if the Lord of the seventh is with a good planet, and the Lord of the fourth is with an evil planet, then it is not good for you to stay there; because if you do continue there, you shall suffer many losses and have evil reports raised against you in that country.

But if you would know when someone who is absent will return, look to the Lord of the *Ascendant*. If you find him in any one of the four *Angles* <note>The 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses, which represent strength and immediate action</note>, he will return within that year. If he is not in an *Angle*, then see how far he is distant from the first *Angle*; for he will stay away for as many years as there are *Houses* between them.

If you wish to be informed of the scarcity or plenty of goods, look to the strong *Houses* <note>the Angles</note>, the *Succedents* <note>Houses 2, 5, 8, and 11</note>, and the *Cadents* <note>Houses 3, 6, 9, and 12</note>. The strong *Houses* signify dearth and scarcity; the *Succedents* signify a moderate season, neither too expensive nor too cheap; and the *Cadents* signify plenty and profitability of things. Consider also the *Planets* and their positions: if they are in strong *Houses*, the things signified by those *Planets* will be rare. Note that *Saturn* signifies fields, vines, and instruments for working in fields, as well as leather; and of fruits, he signifies corn, acorns, oak-apples, and pomegranates.

### Questions of the Sixth House <note>In astrology, the sixth house traditionally governs health, sickness, and service.</note>

Whether the sick shall recover his health or die: If the question concerns his sickness, see if Saturn, or Mars, or the *Dragon’s Tail* <note>term: Dragon's Tail; also known as the South Node of the Moon, traditionally considered a point of misfortune or decrease.</note> is in the first house. If their Lord is joined with an evil planet, then he shall die soon. And if the Lord of the first house is good, but evil planets are in the first with its Lord, or likewise in the first or the eighth house <note>The eighth house is traditionally associated with death.</note>, he will certainly die. But if the Lord of the first is in the eighth, or with the Lord of the eighth; or the Lord of the eighth is in the first, or with the Lord of the first, his death is in doubt. And if evil planets possess the "Angles" <note>term: Angles; the four most powerful houses in a chart (1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th).</note>, evil and destruction are threatened to the sick. But if good planets are in the first, sixth, and eighth, and likewise in the Angles, and the Lord of the first is away from the eighth and its Lord, then the sick person shall live and recover his health.

If you desire to know whether he will be cured by medicines: assign the first house to the Physician, the tenth to the sick person, the seventh to his diseases, and the fourth to the medicines.

# On Astronomical Geomancy

<note>original: "cines" — the end of the word "medicines" from the previous page</note> medicines. If evil planets are in the first house, the physician shall benefit the patient nothing; instead, they testify that the situation will grow worse for the diseased. However, the "Fortunes" <note>Benefic planets, usually Jupiter and Venus</note> signify that the physician shall be profitable to him. And if evil planets occupy the tenth house, the sick person is the cause; for they testify that he himself is the cause of his own disease. But the Fortunes being there signify the contrary. Furthermore, if evil planets are in the tenth house, they change the condition of the sick from one disease into another; but the Fortunes being there deliver him without the help of a physician or medicines. Also, evil planets being in the fourth house testify that the medicines increase his grief; but the Fortunes being there mitigate the pain and heal him.

If you would know if you should go to the person and heal him, consider the astrological place then: for if the house is occupied by *Saturn*, *Mars*, or the *Dragon's Tail* <note>The South Node of the Moon, traditionally considered unlucky</note>, or the *Sun* in retrograde <note>original: "R" — the symbol for retrograde motion</note>, do not go to him. But if she is with an evil planet, and especially in the seventh house, then you shall not go, because you shall benefit him nothing. If there are good planets there, go and look diligently to him, noting in which members he suffers; because *Aries* governs the head, *Taurus* the neck, *Cancer* the breast and lungs, *Leo* the heart and stomach, *Virgo* the belly and intestines, *Libra* the kidneys <note>original: "reins"</note> and loins, *Scorpio* the secret members, *Sagittarius* <note>original: "Sagittary"</note> the thighs, *Capricorn* the knees, *Aquarius* <note>original: "Aquary"</note> the legs, and *Pisces* the feet.

But if the *Sun* and the *Moon* are in the fifth, and if the Lord of the eighth house is with the Lord of the first...
